    The option that says: **Set up IAM user policies to restrict root account capabilities for each member account in AWS Organizations** is incorrect. Although you can use an IAM policy to AWS Organizations, it is more suitable to use a Security Control Policy (SCP) instead. Moreover, setting up an organizational unit (OU) can help you better control your accounts.
    
    The option that says: **Attach a unique External ID to each account. Use AWS Service Catalog to restrict the capabilities of the root user accounts** is incorrect because AWS Service Catalog just allows organizations to create and manage catalogs of IT services that are approved for use on AWS. In addition, attaching an External ID is not suitable in this scenario. This type of ID is only applicable if you want to provision access to an external or 3rd party AWS account.
    
- Enable multi-factor authentication (MFA) of the root user account for each organizational member account.

A startup is testing its in-house application code using an Amazon EC2 instance and recently discovered that the instance was compromised and was serving up malware. Further examination of the instance revealed that it was compromised 30 days ago. Additionally, the startup uses an AWS Elastic Load Balancer (ELB) to distribute incoming traffic to the EC2 instances.

To prevent it from happening again, the startup urgently asked the security engineer to implement a continuous monitoring solution that automatically notifies the security team about compromised instances via email for high-severity findings.

Which combination of steps should the security engineer take to implement the solution? (Select THREE.)

- Activate AWS Security Hub to the AWS account that needs to be monitored.
- **Enable the Amazon GuardDuty service in the AWS account that needs to be monitored.**
- Provision an Amazon EventBridge rule for Security Hub findings with high severity. Configure the rule to publish a message to the queue.
- Provision an Amazon SQS queue, then subscribe the security team's emails to the queue.
- **Create an Amazon EventBridge rule for Amazon GuardDuty findings with high severity. Set up the rule to publish a message on the topic.**
- **Set up an Amazon SNS topic, then subscribe the security team's emails to the topic.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    Amazon GuardDuty creates an event for Amazon EventBridge (Amazon CloudWatch Events) when any change in findings takes place. Finding changes that will create a CloudWatch event include newly generated findings or newly aggregated findings. Events are emitted on a best-effort basis.
    
    Every GuardDuty finding is assigned a finding ID. GuardDuty creates a CloudWatch event for every finding with a unique finding ID. All subsequent occurrences of an existing finding are aggregated to the original finding.
    
    ![GuardDuty Findings](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/scs-c02-guardduty-findings-with-notif.png)
    
    An Amazon EventBridge rule with GuardDuty can be used to set up automated finding alerts by sending GuardDuty finding events to a messaging hub to help increase the visibility of GuardDuty findings. You can send findings alerts to email or Slack by setting up an SNS topic and then connecting that topic to an Amazon EventBridge event rule.
    
    ![GuardDuty & EventBridge](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws_guardduty_eventbridge_jul2023.png)
    
    Users can create a custom event pattern with the EventBridge rule to match a specific finding type. Then, route the response to an Amazon Simple Notification Service (Amazon SNS) topic.
    
    Therefore, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Enable the Amazon GuardDuty service in the AWS account that needs to be monitored.**
    
    **– Set up an Amazon SNS topic, then subscribe the security team’s emails to the topic.**
    
    **– Create an Amazon EventBridge rule for Amazon GuardDuty findings with high severity. Set up the rule to publish a message on the topic.**
    
    The option that says: **Activate AWS Security Hub to the AWS account that needs to be monitored** is incorrect because AWS Security Hub does not directly perform real-time monitoring of AWS resources. Instead, it simply acts as a central aggregation and visualization point for security findings generated by various AWS security services and integrated third-party security tools. These security findings come from AWS GuardDuty, Amazon Inspector, Amazon Macie, AWS Config, AWS Firewall Manager, and more.
    
    The option that says: **Provision an Amazon SQS queue, then subscribe the security team’s emails to the queue** is incorrect because Amazon SQS is primarily used for message queuing. It does not offer notification capability; thus, you should use Amazon SNS instead.
    
    The option that says: **Provision an Amazon EventBridge rule for Security Hub findings with high severity. Configure the rule to publish a message to the queue** is incorrect because AWS Security Hub does not directly support filtering security findings based on Amazon EventBridge events.
    

A Security Administrator has been instructed to set up a solution to easily control access to the data being encrypted under an AWS KMS key. The solution must use additional authenticated data (AAD) to support authenticated encryption and avoid tampering the ciphertext.

What is the BEST solution in this scenario that the Administrator should implement?

- Create a key alias and use it when calling the `Encrypt` and `Decrypt` API actions.
- **Add a `kms:EncryptionContext` condition when defining the key policy for the KMS key.**
    
    All **AWS KMS** cryptographic operations with symmetric encryption KMS keys accept an encryption context, an optional set of key-value pairs that can contain additional contextual information about the data. AWS KMS uses the encryption context as additional authenticated data (AAD) to support authenticated encryption.
    
    You cannot specify an encryption context in a cryptographic operation with an asymmetric KMS key. The standard asymmetric encryption algorithms that AWS KMS uses do not support an encryption context.
    
    When you include an encryption context in an encryption request, it is cryptographically bound to the ciphertext such that the same encryption context is required to decrypt (or decrypt and re-encrypt) the data. If the encryption context provided in the decryption request is not an exact, case-sensitive match, the decrypt request fails. Only the order of the key-value pairs in the encryption context can vary.
    
    ![AWS KMS cryptographic operations with symmetric encryption KMS keys](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws-kms-encryption-context-sample.jpg)
    
    The encryption context is not secret. It appears in plaintext in AWS CloudTrail Logs so you can use it to identify and categorize your cryptographic operations.
    
    An encryption context can consist of any keys and values. However, because it is not secret and not encrypted, your encryption context should not include sensitive information. It is recommended that your encryption context describe the data being encrypted or decrypted. For example, when you encrypt a file, you might use part of the file path as encryption context.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Add a `kms:EncryptionContext` condition when defining the key policy for the KMS key.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a key alias and use it when calling the `Encrypt` and `Decrypt` API actions**is incorrect because an alias is just an optional display name for a KMS key. It doesn’t enable additional authenticated data (AAD) to support authenticated encryption.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS CloudHSM instead since KMS does not support authenticated encryption** is incorrect because you can use encryption context in AWS KMS as an additional authenticated data (AAD) to support authenticated encryption. Therefore, this statement is wrong.
    
    The option that says: **Use `Grant` tokens to enable authenticated encryption** is incorrect. Although you can use *grants* with encryption context, a *grant* token serves a different purpose. This is primarily used to mitigate the potential delay when creating a *grant*. A grant token is included in the response you get from your CreateGrant request which you can use with the `RetireGrant API.`
    
- Use `Grant` tokens to enable authenticated encryption.
- Use AWS CloudHSM instead since KMS does not support authenticated encryption.

A company has a multitier online application hosted in several Amazon EC2 instances that is publicly accessible around the world. The Security Administrator has already placed the required network access control lists and security groups in the Amazon VPC of the application. The web servers are hosted in public subnets behind a public-facing Application Load Balancer, while the application servers are hosted in private subnets. The Administrator needs to enhance the edge security of the cloud architecture to safeguard the EC2 instances against attacks.

Which combination of options should be implemented in this scenario? (Select TWO.)

- Use a NAT Gateway for all the inbound traffic to the application.
- **Integrate AWS WAF to the Application Load Balancer to provide SQL injection or cross-site scripting attack protection to the online application. Launch a new Amazon CloudFront distribution and configure it to use AWS WAF.**
- **Migrate the web servers to private subnets without any public IP or Elastic IP addresses.**
- Launch a new Amazon CloudFront distribution and configure geo-restriction to prevent users in specific geographic locations from accessing content.
- Establish an AWS Direct Connect connection with a private virtual interface (VIF) to securely route all incoming traffic to the Amazon VPC without traversing the public Internet.
- **Evaluation**
    
    **AWS WAF** is a web application firewall that helps protect your web applications or APIs against common web exploits that may affect availability, compromise security, or consume excessive resources. AWS WAF gives you control over how traffic reaches your applications by enabling you to create security rules that block common attack patterns, such as SQL injection or cross-site scripting, and rules that filter out specific traffic patterns you define. You can get started quickly using Managed Rules for AWS WAF, a pre-configured set of rules managed by AWS or AWS Marketplace Sellers. The Managed Rules for WAF address issues like the OWASP Top 10 security risks. These rules are regularly updated as new issues emerge. AWS WAF includes a full-featured API that you can use to automate the creation, deployment, and maintenance of security rules.
    
    ![AWS WAF](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/product-page-diagram_AWS-WAF_How-it-Works_3SEP2023.png)
    
    AWS WAF is tightly integrated with Amazon CloudFront and the Application Load Balancer (ALB), services that AWS customers commonly use to deliver content for their websites and applications. When you use AWS WAF on Amazon CloudFront, your rules run in all AWS Edge Locations, located around the world close to your end-users. This means security doesn’t come at the expense of performance. Blocked requests are stopped before they reach your web servers. When you use AWS WAF on Application Load Balancer, your rules run in region and can be used to protect internet-facing as well as internal load balancers.
    
    In this scenario, the web servers are hosted in public subnets behind a public-facing Application Load Balancer while the application servers are hosted in private subnets. To better protect your web servers against direct attacks, you can migrate your servers to private subnets and then remove any attached public IP or Elastic IP addresses. The public-facing Application Load Balancer can route the traffic to these web servers hosted in private subnets. This will also significantly reduce the attack surface of your cloud infrastructure.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Migrate the web servers to private subnets without any public IP or Elastic IP addresses.**
    
    **– Integrate AWS WAF to the Application Load Balancer to provide SQL injection or cross-site scripting attack protection to the online application. Launch a new Amazon CloudFront distribution and configure it to use AWS WAF.**
    
    The option that says: **Use a NAT Gateway for all the inbound traffic to the application** is incorrect because a NAT gateway is typically used to enable instances in a private subnet to connect to the Internet or other AWS services, but prevent the Internet from initiating a connection with those instances.
    
    The option that says: **Launch a new Amazon CloudFront distribution and configure geo-restriction to prevent users in specific geographic locations from accessing content** is incorrect. Although CloudFront is part of the Amazon CloudFront Global Edge Network, using geo-restriction is not a suitable feature in this scenario. The security attack could originate from any country and the scenario doesn’t allude to anything about restricting access to your content based on the geographic location of your viewers.
    
    The option that says: **Establish an AWS Direct Connect connection with a private virtual interface (VIF) to securely route all incoming traffic to the Amazon VPC without traversing the public Internet** is incorrect because AWS Direct Connect with a private VIF is primarily designed for private, high-bandwidth connections between an on-premises data center and AWS. It does not handle or secure publicly accessible EC2 instances against external threats such as DDoS attacks, SQL injection, or cross-site scripting. Additionally, Direct Connect does not provide edge security features like AWS WAF, CloudFront, or Shield to protect internet-facing applications.
    

An organization is planning to launch an application that will store sensitive files in three Amazon S3 buckets based on a data classification scheme of `Restricted`, `Private`, and `Public`. The solution must encrypt each object using a unique key, and the AWS KMS must be set to automatically rotate encryption keys annually. Moreover, access to files in the `Restricted` bucket must be protected by two-factor authentication.

Which of the following solutions will satisfy the above requirement?

- Create a Customer Managed key for each data classification type and enable the rotation of keys annually. Encrypt the files using Server-Side Encryption with a KMS key Stored in AWS Key Management Service (SSE-KMS). Configure MFA (multi-factor authentication) delete on the `Restricted` bucket.
- **Create a Customer Managed key for each data classification type. Enable the rotation of keys annually. Set up an MFA policy within the key policy for the `Restricted` KMS key. Encrypt the files using Server-Side Encryption with a KMS key Stored in AWS Key Management Service (SSE-KMS).**
    
    Cryptography standards advise against the repeated use of encryption keys for security reasons. To refresh the cryptographic material for your KMS key, you have two options: (1) create a new key and update your application or alias to use it, or (2) enable automatic key rotation for a customer-managed key that you already have in place.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/key-rotation-auto_7SEP2023.png)
    
    When you enable *automatic key rotation* for a Customer Managed key, AWS KMS generates new cryptographic material for the KMS key every year. AWS KMS also saves the key’s older cryptographic material in perpetuity so it can be used to decrypt data that is encrypted. AWS KMS does not delete any rotated key material until you delete the KMS key.
    
    Key rotation changes only the KMS key’s *backing key*, which is the cryptographic material that is used in encryption operations. The KMS key is the same logical resource, regardless of whether or how frequently its backing key changes. The properties of the KMS key do not change.
    
    Automatic key rotation is not eligible for keys in custom key stores and keys with imported key material.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a Customer Managed key for each data classification type. Enable the rotation of keys annually. Set up an MFA policy within the key policy for the `Restricted` KMS key. Encrypt the files using Server-Side Encryption with a KMS key Stored in AWS Key Management Service (SSE-KMS).**
    
    The option that says: **Create a Customer Managed key for each data classification type with `aws:MultiFactorAuthPresent` and `kms:EnableKeyRotation` elements set to true. Configure a policy that will allow Amazon S3 to use the grants to encrypt each file with a unique KMS key** is incorrect because the scenario says that the `Restricted` bucket is the only one required to have two-factor authentication to decrypt the data. In addition, using grants is only suitable if there is a requirement to programmatically delegate the use of KMS key to other AWS principals.
    
    The option that says: **Create a Customer Managed key for each data classification type and enable the rotation of keys annually. Encrypt the files using Server-Side Encryption with a KMS key Stored in AWS Key Management Service (SSE-KMS). Configure MFA (multi-factor authentication) delete on the `Restricted` bucket** is incorrect because MFA delete only provides protection against object version deletion, not against other types of unauthorized access such as reading, writing, or modifying S3 objects.
    
    The option that says: **Create a Customer Managed key with unique imported key material for each data classification type. Enable the rotation of keys annually. Define the MFA policy in the key policy for the `Restricted` key material. Encrypt the files using Server-Side Encryption with a KMS key Stored in AWS Key Management Service (SSE-KMS)** is incorrect. Although using SSE-KMS is possible, you cannot allow automatic key rotations for KMS key with unique imported key material.
    
- Create a Customer Managed Key with unique imported key material for each data classification type. Enable the rotation of keys annually. Define the MFA policy in the key policy for the `Restricted` key material. Encrypt the files using Server-Side Encryption with a KMS key Stored in AWS Key Management Service (SSE-KMS).
- Create a Customer Managed key for each data classification type with `aws:MultiFactorAuthPresent` and `kms:EnableKeyRotation` elements set to true. Configure a policy that will allow Amazon S3 to use the grants to encrypt each file with a unique KMS key.

The Security team wants to delegate user creation duties to the SysOps Administrator. However, the team must ensure that the SysOps Administrator creates users that adhere to the following company rules:

– Users cannot use IAM to create or manage users, groups, roles, or policies.

– Users are denied access to the Amazon S3 logs bucket and cannot access production EC2 instances.

How can the team fulfill this task?

- Delegate the user creation duties using Service Control Policies (SCPs).
- Delegate the user creation duties using Resource-Based Policies.
- Delegate the user creation duties using Session Policies.
- **Delegate the user creation duties using Permissions Boundaries.**
    
    You manage access in AWS by creating policies and attaching them to IAM identities (users, groups of users, or roles) or AWS resources. A policy is an object in AWS that, when associated with an identity or resource, defines their permissions. AWS evaluates these policies when an IAM principal (user or role) makes a request. Permissions in the policies determine whether the request is allowed or denied. Most policies are stored in AWS as JSON documents. AWS supports six types of policies: identity-based policies, resource-based policies, permissions boundaries, Organizations SCPs, ACLs, and session policies.
    
    AWS supports *permissions boundaries* for IAM entities (users or roles). A permissions boundary is an advanced feature for using a managed policy to set the maximum permissions that an identity-based policy can grant to an IAM entity. An entity’s permissions boundary allows it to perform only the actions that are allowed by both its identity-based policies and its permissions boundaries.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/permissions_boundary_9SEP2023.png)
    
    You can use permissions boundaries to delegate permissions management tasks, such as user creation, to IAM users in your account. This permits others to perform tasks on your behalf within a specific boundary of permissions.
    
    For example, assume that María is the administrator of the X-Company AWS account. She wants to delegate user creation duties to Zhang. However, she must ensure that Zhang creates users that adhere to the following company rules:
    
    – Users cannot use IAM to create or manage users, groups, roles, or policies.
    
    – Users are denied access to the Amazon S3 `logs` bucket and cannot access the `i-1234567890abcdef0` Amazon EC2 instance.
    
    – Users cannot remove their own boundary policies.
    
    To enforce these rules, María completes the following tasks, for which details are included below:
    
    1. María creates the `XCompanyBoundaries` managed policy to use as a permissions boundary for all new users in the account.
    2. María creates the `DelegatedUserBoundary` managed policy and assigns it as the permissions boundary for Zhang. Maria makes a note of her admin IAM user’s ARN and uses it in the policy to prevent Zhang from accessing it.
    3. María creates the `DelegatedUserPermissions` managed policy and attaches it as a permissions policy for Zhang.
    4. María tells Zhang about his new responsibilities and limitations.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Delegate the user creation duties using Permissions Boundaries.**
    
    The option that says: **Delegate the user creation duties using Service Control Policies (SCPs)** is incorrect because SCPs simply define the maximum permissions for account members of an organization or organizational unit (OU). SCPs limit permissions that identity-based policies or resource-based policies grant to entities (users or roles) within the account, but do not grant permissions.
    
    The option that says: **Delegate the user creation duties using Resource-Based Policies** is incorrect because Resource-based policies just grant permissions to the principal that is specified in the policy.
    
    The option that says: **Delegate the user creation duties using Session Policies** is incorrect because this solution only passes advanced session policies when you use the AWS CLI or AWS API to assume a role or a federated user. Session policies only limit the permissions that the role or user’s identity-based policies grant to the session.
    

An organization has a hybrid cloud infrastructure spanning AWS and its data center connected by a Site-to-Site VPN. The organization uses Amazon Route 53 Resolver forwarding rules to manage its DNS traffic, both for authoritative and non-authoritative domains. The organization must log all the DNS traffic that originates from its AWS resources and be able to query the DNS name that was requested in the request. The solution must also be queryable to identify the source IP address that the query originated from.

Which of the following is the MOST suitable solution to satisfy this requirement?

- Turn on DNS logging for the accelerated Site-to-Site VPN connection. Send the DNS logs to an Amazon S3 bucket and use Amazon Athena to query the DNS traffic.
- Capture network traffic using VPC Traffic Mirroring. Set the relevant elastic network interfaces as the mirror source. Enable `amazon-dns` in the mirror filter settings and configure Amazon CloudWatch Logs as the mirror target. Use CloudWatch Insights to query the DNS name and source IP address.
- **Set up query logging for relevant Amazon VPCs within the company using Amazon Route 53 Resolver. Forward the logs to Amazon CloudWatch Logs and examine the DNS data utilizing Amazon CloudWatch Logs Insights.**
    
    Amazon Route 53 Resolver responds recursively to DNS queries from AWS resources for public records, Amazon VPC-specific DNS names, and Amazon Route 53 private hosted zones and is available by default in all VPCs.
    
    As is standard for DNS resolvers, resolvers cache DNS queries for a length of time determined by the time-to-live (TTL) for the resolver. The Route 53 Resolver caches queries that originate in your VPCs, and responds from the cache whenever possible to speed up responses. Resolver query logging logs only unique queries, not queries that Resolver is able to respond to from the cache.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/amazon-route-53-query-logging.png)
    
    For example, suppose that an EC2 instance in one of the VPCs that a query logging configuration is logging queries for submits a request for accounting.example.com. Resolver caches the response to that query and logs the query. If the same instance’s elastic network interface makes a query for accounting.example.com within the TTL of the Resolver’s cache, Resolver responds to the query from the cache. The second query is not logged.
    
    You can log the following DNS queries:
    
    – Queries that originate in Amazon Virtual Private Cloud VPCs that you specify, as well as the responses to those DNS queries.
    
    – Queries from on-premises resources that use an inbound Resolver endpoint.
    
    – Queries that use an outbound Resolver endpoint for recursive DNS resolution.
    
    – Queries that use Route 53 Resolver DNS Firewall rules to block, allow, or monitor domain lists.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up query logging for relevant Amazon VPCs within the company using Amazon Route 53 Resolver. Forward the logs to Amazon CloudWatch Logs and examine the DNS data utilizing Amazon CloudWatch Logs Insights.**
    
    The option that says: **Turn on DNS logging for the accelerated Site-to-Site VPN connection. Send the DNS logs to an Amazon S3 bucket and use Amazon Athena to query the DNS traffic** is incorrect. Site-to-Site VPN does not have a DNS logging feature that can satisfy the given scenario. The more suitable solution here is to use the Route 53 Resolver query logging to track all the DNS traffic.
    
    The option that says: **Capture network traffic using VPC Traffic Mirroring. Set the relevant elastic network interfaces as the mirror source. Enable `amazon-dns` in the mirror filter settings and configure Amazon CloudWatch Logs as the mirror target. Use CloudWatch Insights to query the DNS name and source IP address** is incorrect. Amazon CloudWatch Logs is not a valid mirror target for VPC Traffic Mirroring. A mirror target can be an elastic network interface, a network load balancer, or a gateway load balancer endpoint.
    
    The option that says: **Create an Amazon OpenSearch Service domain and direct flow logs from all Amazon VPCs to an Amazon Data Firehose stream. Configure the Data Firehose stream to send the logs to the OpenSearch domain and enable querying of IP address and DNS name information** is incorrect because VPC Flow Logs is simply a feature that enables you to capture information about the IP traffic going to and from network interfaces in your Amazon VPC. This feature is not capable of tracking DNS traffic from your on-premises network to AWS.
    
- Create an Amazon OpenSearch Service domain and direct flow logs from all Amazon VPCs to an Amazon Data Firehose stream. Configure the Data Firehose stream to send the logs to the OpenSearch domain and enable querying of IP address and DNS name information.

An organization is heavily using AWS KMS to encrypt the data of its application. A Security Engineer has been instructed to analyze suspicious KMS API events but is overwhelmed by the volume of audit logs being produced in AWS CloudTrail. Certain AWS KMS actions such as `Encrypt`, `Decrypt`, and `GenerateDataKey` typically generate more than 99% of the events. The Engineer is only tracking certain events such as `Disable`, `Delete`, and `ScheduleKey` actions.

Which of the following is the MOST efficient solution that will allow the Engineer to track and search through the audit logs?

- Set up Amazon S3 event notifications to track all `Disable`, `Delete`, and `ScheduleKey`actions in AWS KMS.
- Enable Amazon Macie in the S3 bucket that contains the CloudTrail audit logs to classify and filter all relevant KMS API actions.
- **Create a trail in AWS CloudTrail and choose a separate S3 bucket to receive the audit log files. Configure the trail to record write-only management events in the bucket.**
    
    **AWS CloudTrail** is an AWS service that helps you enable governance, compliance, and operational and risk auditing of your AWS account. Actions taken by a user, role, or an AWS service are recorded as events in CloudTrail. Events include actions taken in the AWS Management Console, AWS Command Line Interface, and AWS SDKs and APIs. Visibility into your AWS account activity is a key aspect of security and operational best practices. You can use CloudTrail to view, search, download, archive, analyze, and respond to account activity across your AWS infrastructure. You can identify who or what took which action, what resources were acted upon, when the event occurred, and other details to help you analyze and respond to activity in your AWS account. Optionally, you can enable AWS CloudTrail Insights on a trail to help you identify and respond to unusual activity.
    
    You can integrate CloudTrail into applications using the API, automate trail creation for your organization, check the status of trails you create, and control how users view CloudTrail events.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/write-only-image-management_10SEP2023.png)
    
    Management events provide visibility into management operations that are performed on resources in your AWS account. These are also known as control plane operations. Example management events include: configuring security (for example, IAM AttachRolePolicy API operations), registering devices (for example, Amazon EC2 CreateDefaultVpc API operations), configuring rules for routing data (for example, Amazon EC2 CreateSubnet API operations) and setting up logging (for example, AWS CloudTrail CreateTrail API operations). Management events can also include non-API events that occur in your account. For example, when a user logs in to your account, CloudTrail logs the ConsoleLogin event.
    
    When you configure your trail to log management events, you can specify whether you want read-only events, write-only events, both, or none.
    
    **Read-only** – Logs API operations that read your resources, but don’t make changes. For example, read-only events include the Amazon EC2 DescribeSecurityGroups and DescribeSubnets API operations. These operations return only information about your Amazon EC2 resources and don’t change your configurations.
    
    **Write-only** – Logs API operations that modify (or might modify) your resources. For example, the Amazon EC2 RunInstances and TerminateInstances API operations modify your instances.
    
    **All** – Logs API operations that read and modify your resources.
    
    **None** – Disables logging of read-only and write-only management events.
    
    AWS KMS actions such as Encrypt, Decrypt, and GenerateDataKey typically generate a large volume (more than 99%) of events. These actions are logged as **Read** events. Low-volume, relevant AWS KMS actions such as Disable, Delete, and ScheduleKey (which typically account for less than 0.5% of AWS KMS event volume) are logged as **Write** events.
    
    To exclude high-volume events like Encrypt, Decrypt, and GenerateDataKey, but still log relevant events such as Disable, Delete, and ScheduleKey, you can configure the trail to log Write-only management events.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a trail in AWS CloudTrail and choose a separate S3 bucket to receive the audit log files. Configure the trail to record write-only management events in the bucket.**
    
    The option that says: **Enable Amazon Macie in the S3 bucket that contains the CloudTrail audit logs to classify and filter all relevant KMS API actions** is incorrect because Amazon Macie is primarily used to automatically discover, classify, and protect sensitive data in AWS. Amazon Macie recognizes sensitive data such as personally identifiable information (PII) or intellectual property. It is not a suitable service to be used to filter CloudTrail audit logs that are hosted in the S3 bucket.
    
    The option that says: **In the AWS CloudTrail console, create a new trail and select a separate S3 bucket to receive the audit log files. Set the trail to record read-only management events in the bucket** is incorrect because the Read-only option logs the API operations that just read your resources, but don’t make changes. You have to configure the trail to log Write-only management events in order to effectively track the `Disable`, `Delete` and `ScheduleKey` actions in AWS KMS.
    
    The option that says: **Set up Amazon S3 event notifications to track all `Disable`, `Delete` and `ScheduleKey` actions in AWS KMS** is incorrect because the Amazon S3 notification feature simply enables you to receive notifications when certain events happen in your bucket only, and not in AWS CloudTrail.
    
- In the AWS CloudTrail console, create a new trail and select a separate S3 bucket to receive the audit log files. Set the trail to record read-only management events in the bucket.

A company is planning to shift its operations to AWS. The security lead is in charge of running continuous security checks on resource configurations against company-defined policies as well as the Center for Internet Security’s (CIS) AWS Foundations Benchmarks.

How can the security lead fulfill these requirements?

- **Enable AWS Config across all regions and set it to track all resource configuration changes. Turn on AWS Security Hub and ensure that CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark is enabled.**
    
    **CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark** is a set of security configuration best practices for AWS. These industry-accepted best practices go beyond the high-level security guidance already available, providing AWS users with clear, step-by-step implementation and assessment procedures.
    
    **AWS Security Hub** provides you with a comprehensive view of your security state in AWS and helps you check your environment against security industry standards including AWS Foundational Security Best Practices, CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark, and Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard (PCI DSS)
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws-security-hub-CIS-AWS-benchmark.JPG)
    
    Enabling AWS Security Hub will assist you in monitoring and testing whether your AWS configurations comply with the CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark and other best practices. Keep in mind that AWS Security Hub performs security checks using service-linked AWS Config rules, so ensure that resource recording is enabled in AWS Config first.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Enable AWS Config across all regions and set it to track all resource configuration changes. Turn on AWS Security Hub and ensure that CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark is enabled.**
    
    The option that says: **Enable AWS Config across all regions and set it to track all resource configuration changes. Turn on Amazon Inspector and ensure that CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark is enabled** is incorrect. Though the use of AWS Config is correct, Amazon Inspector only works with EC2 instances to detect software vulnerabilities and unintended network exposure. It cannot evaluate the configuration of AWS resources based on the CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark.
    
    The option that says: **Allow Amazon Inspector to scan all Regions for potential vulnerabilities. Use Amazon Macie to assess whether the findings violate the CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark** is incorrect. Amazon Macie is only used for identifying sensitive information on files stored in Amazon S3.
    
    The option that says: **Allow Amazon Inspector to scan all Regions for potential vulnerabilities. Use AWS Security Hub to assess whether the findings violate the CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark** is incorrect. AWS Config needs to be enabled first for the AWS Security Hub to work.
    
- Allow Amazon Inspector to scan all Regions for potential vulnerabilities. Use AWS Security Hub to assess whether the findings violate the CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark.
- Enable AWS Config across all regions and set it to track all resource configuration changes. Turn on Amazon Inspector and ensure that CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark is enabled.
- Allow Amazon Inspector to scan all Regions for potential vulnerabilities. Use Amazon Macie to assess whether the findings violate the CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark.

A company intends to establish host-based security measures for its Amazon EC2 instances and containers stored within the Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR). The company has already installed the AWS Systems Manager Agent (SSM Agent) on its EC2 instances. All of the company’s AWS accounts are under a single organization within AWS Organizations and are integrated using AWS Control Tower. The primary objective is to assess the workloads for potential software vulnerabilities and any inadvertent network exposure.

The company plans to forward any findings to AWS Security Hub, a platform already configured for the organization. This solution must be deployed automatically across all member accounts, including newly created ones. Furthermore, it must possess the capability to perform workload scans promptly as new workloads become operational.

Which solution would best satisfy these specified requirements?

- **Designate a delegated administrator for Amazon Inspector. Enable the option to automatically activate Amazon Inspector for new member accounts.**
    
    Amazon Inspector is a vulnerability management service that continuously scans your AWS workloads for software vulnerabilities and unintended network exposure. Amazon Inspector automatically discovers and scans running Amazon EC2 instances, container images in Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR), and AWS Lambda functions for known software vulnerabilities and unintended network exposure.
    
    Amazon Inspector can provide Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVE) data for your EC2 instances, but you have to ensure that the Amazon EC2 Systems Manager (SSM) agent is installed and activated. This agent is preinstalled on many EC2 instances, but you might need to activate it manually. Regardless of SSM agent status, all of your EC2 instances are scanned for network exposure issues.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Amazon_Inspector_activate_to_member_accounts_BJL_29082023.png)
    
    Security Hub provides a comprehensive view of your security state in AWS and helps you check your environment against security industry standards and best practices. Amazon Inspector integration with Security Hub allows you to send findings from Amazon Inspector to Security Hub. Security Hub can then include those findings in its analysis of your security posture.
    
    As a delegated administrator for your organization, you can activate Amazon EC2 scanning, Amazon ECR scanning, or both, for any member associated with the AWS Organizations management account. When you activate scans for a member account, that account becomes associated with the delegated administrator, Amazon Inspector is automatically activated, and scans of the chosen type are started immediately.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is:  **Designate a delegated administrator for Amazon Inspector. Enable the option to automatically activate Amazon Inspector for new member accounts.**
    
    The option that says: **Designate a delegated administrator for Amazon Inspector. Create an Amazon EventBridge rule for all AWS Security Hub’s EC2 and ECR findings. Notify the company’s security team using Amazon SNS as the rule target** is incorrect. This implementation can help the company to be aware of the different types of security findings, allowing them to take action; however, this solution does not satisfy the requirements to accommodate automated scans for new workloads. It is right to use Amazon Inspector, but you need to activate automatic scanning for all new member accounts.
    
    The option that says: **Designate a delegated administrator for Amazon GuardDuty. Activate auto-enable for all new member accounts** is incorrect. While this is the right way to manage Amazon GuardDuty integrated with AWS Organizations, the fact still remains that GuardDuty is primarily used for threat detection and not for assessing workloads for potential software vulnerabilities and any inadvertent network exposure. The Amazon GuardDuty service is not the best service for this type of scenario. A better solution that you should use is Amazon Inspector instead.
    
    The option that says: **Designate a delegated administrator for Amazon Inspector. Install the Amazon Inspector agent to all EC2 instances to allow automatic EC2 scanning** is incorrect because you do not need to install the Amazon Inspector agent in order to implement automatic EC2 scanning. When AWS Systems Manager Agent (SSM Agent) is installed in the EC2 instances, you can utilize this feature.
    
- Designate a delegated administrator for Amazon GuardDuty. Activate auto-enable for all new member accounts.
- Designate a delegated administrator for Amazon Inspector. Create an Amazon EventBridge rule for all AWS Security Hub's EC2 and ECR findings. Notify the company's security team using Amazon SNS as the rule target.
- Designate a delegated administrator for Amazon Inspector. Install the Amazon Inspector agent to all EC2 instances to allow automatic EC2 scanning.

A software company involved in cryptocurrencies is exploring Bitcoin mining technologies on AWS. The Amazon GuardDuty was activated by the company’s security team but is troubled by the high number of generated findings across its AWS accounts. The goal is to reduce the occurrence of false positives in GuardDuty’s detection of compromised instances involved in mining activities and significantly reduce the management overhead.

What would be the best way to achieve this while still being able to use GuardDuty?

- Create a suppression rule in GuardDuty Console. Filter by finding the type `CryptoCurrency:EC2/BitcoinTool.B!DNS` and by instance id of the instances running mining activities.
- Tag the Amazon EC2 instance with `Mining`. Create an Amazon EventBridge rule triggered by GuardDuty finding type `CryptoCurrency:EC2/BitcoinTool.B!DNS`. Create a custom lambda to extract instances using the tag `Mining`. Notify the security team.
- **Tag the Amazon EC2 instances with `Mining`. Create a suppression rule in GuardDuty Console. Filter by finding the type `CryptoCurrency:EC2/BitcoinTool.B!DNS` and by instance tag value `Mining`.**
    
    Amazon GuardDuty is a security monitoring service that analyzes and processes Foundational data sources, such as AWS CloudTrail management events, AWS CloudTrail event logs, VPC flow logs (from Amazon EC2 instances), and DNS logs. One of the features of Amazon GuardDuty is the suppression rule. A suppression rule is a set of criteria consisting of a filter attribute paired with a value used to filter findings by automatically archiving new findings that match the specified criteria.
    
    Suppression rules can be used to filter low-value findings, false positive findings, or threats you do not intend to act on to make it easier to recognize the security threats with the most impact on your environment. A sample use case is suppressing the `CryptoCurrency:EC2/BitcoinTool.B!DNS` finding type. This finding informs you that the listed EC2 instance in your AWS environment is querying a domain name that is associated with Bitcoin or other cryptocurrency-related activity. If you use this EC2 instance to mine or manage cryptocurrency, or this instance is otherwise involved in blockchain activity, this finding could be an expected activity for your environment. If this is the case in your AWS environment, AWS recommends that you set up a suppression rule for this finding.
    
    ![Suppression rule](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/guardduty_supression_rule_BJL_20062023.png)
    
    For this scenario, you can create a suppression rule that allows you to filter out the specific finding type `CryptoCurrency:EC2/BitcoinTool.B!DNS` and Instance tag value. Utilizing tags provides more flexibility in managing instances involved in mining activities and allows for easier maintenance and scalability as your infrastructure grows. It simplifies the process of applying the suppression rule to relevant instances and reduces the risk of missing instances.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Tag the Amazon EC2 instances with `Mining`. Create a suppression rule in GuardDuty Console. Filter by finding the type `CryptoCurrency:EC2/BitcoinTool.B!DNS` and by instance tag value `Mining`.**
    
    The option that says: **Tag the Amazon EC2 instance with `Mining`. Create an Amazon EventBridge rule triggered by GuardDuty finding type `CryptoCurrency:EC2/BitcoinTool.B!DNS`. Create a custom lambda to extract instances using the tag `Mining`. Notify the security team** is incorrect. Although this will primarily help streamline the response to GuardDuty findings and enables a faster and more efficient incident management process, it still doesn’t directly address the issue of false positives. False positives can still occur in Amazon GuardDuty without using a suppression rule.
    
    The option that says: **Create an Amazon EventBridge rule triggered by GuardDuty finding type `Execution:EC2/MaliciousFile`. Create an AWS Lambda function that automatically isolates the affected instance by modifying its security group to deny all outbound traffic** is incorrect because the question is specifically about reducing false positives for Bitcoin mining detections (`CryptoCurrency:EC2/BitcoinTool.B!DNS`), not handling generic malware execution (`Execution:EC2/MaliciousFile`). Additionally, isolating an instance using security group modifications is just an incident response measure, not a suppression mechanism, to reduce unwanted GuardDuty findings.
    
    The option that says: **Create a suppression rule in GuardDuty Console. Filter by finding the type `CryptoCurrency:EC2/BitcoinTool.B!DNS` and by instance id of the instances running mining activities** is incorrect. Even though the use of a suppression rule is right, it still requires manual management of the instance IDs. If there are frequent changes to the instances or if new instances are added to the mining activities, you will need to update the suppression rule with the latest instance IDs. Failure to update the suppression rule may result in false positives being generated for those instances, which leads to a higher management overhead.
    
- Create an Amazon EventBridge rule triggered by GuardDuty finding type `Execution:EC2/MaliciousFile`. Create an AWS Lambda function that automatically isolates the affected instance by modifying its security group to deny all outbound traffic.

A company has a suite of web applications deployed on hundreds of Amazon EC2 instances in AWS. The Security Team has been tasked to verify that the security groups and network access control lists (network ACLs) are properly configured and work as expected.

What step should the team do to fulfill this task?

- Set up an AWS Config rule for each security group and network ACL configuration. Send and store the logs to an S3 bucket then use Amazon Inspector to query the network logs.
- Enable AWS CloudTrail in the AWS Account. View and analyze the network traffic going in and out of the VPC using the Event History of the CloudTrail console.
- **Enable VPC Flow Logs to capture information about the IP traffic going to and from network interfaces in your VPC. Store the logs in Amazon S3 and use Amazon Athena to query and analyze the logs.**
    
    **VPC Flow Logs** is a feature that enables you to capture information about the IP traffic going to and from network interfaces in your VPC. Flow log data can be published to Amazon CloudWatch Logs and Amazon S3. After you’ve created a flow log, you can retrieve and view its data in the chosen destination.
    
    Flow logs can help you with a number of tasks; for example, to troubleshoot why specific traffic is not reaching an instance, which in turn helps you diagnose overly restrictive security group rules. You can also use flow logs as a security tool to monitor the traffic that is reaching your instance.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/VPC-Flow-Logs.png)
    
    Amazon Athena is an interactive query service that makes it easy to analyze data in Amazon S3 using standard SQL. Athena is serverless, so there is no infrastructure to manage, and you pay only for the queries that you run. Athena is also easy to use. Simply point to your data in Amazon S3, define the schema, and start querying using standard SQL. Most results are delivered within seconds. With Athena, there’s no need for complex ETL jobs to prepare your data for analysis. This makes it easy for anyone with SQL skills to quickly analyze large-scale datasets.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Enable VPC Flow Logs to capture information about the IP traffic going to and from network interfaces in your VPC. Store the logs in Amazon S3 and use Amazon Athena to query and analyze the logs.**
    
    The option that says: **Set up an AWS Config rule for each security group and network ACL configuration. Send and store the logs to an S3 bucket then use Amazon Inspector to query the network logs** is incorrect because AWS Config just continuously monitors and records your AWS resource configurations and allows you to automate the evaluation of recorded configurations against desired configurations. It is not capable of capturing the network information about the IP traffic going to and from your VPC. In addition, Amazon Inspector is only an automated security assessment service and is not capable of querying the network logs.
    
    The option that says: **Enable AWS CloudTrail in the AWS Account. View and analyze the network traffic going in and out of the VPC using the Event History of the CloudTrail console** is incorrect because CloudTrail is primarily used to log, continuously monitor, and retain account activity related to actions across your AWS infrastructure. It is not capable of tracking the IP traffic in your VPC.
    
    The option that says: **Set up and install the Amazon Inspector agent on all EC2 instances using the Systems Manager Run Command to capture the IP traffic. Store the network logs in Amazon S3 and use Amazon Athena to query and analyze the logs** is incorrect because AWS Inspector is just an automated security assessment service that helps you test the security state of your applications running on Amazon EC2.
    
- Set up and install the Amazon Inspector agent on all EC2 instances using the Systems Manager Run Command to capture the IP traffic. Store the network logs in Amazon S3 and use Amazon Athena to query and analyze the logs.

A company is in the process of creating a new AWS account where it will deploy its containerized applicate suite. Its security engineer is tasked to consistently oversee the AWS account by setting up automated compliance checks that are aligned with the Center for Internet Security (CIS) AWS Foundations Benchmarks and other AWS best practices.

How can the engineer fulfill the requirement with the LEAST operational overhead using AWS services?

- Turn on AWS Config and configure it to record all the resources in all regions and global resources. Deploy Operational Best Practices for CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark conformance pack in AWS Config.
- Activate Amazon Inspector and configure it to scan resources based on the Center for Internet Security's CIS Benchmarks in all regions. Enable AWS Security Hub and select AWS Foundational Security Best Practices (FSBP) to enable the standard.
- **Turn on AWS Config and configure it to record all the resources in all regions and global resources. Enable AWS Security Hub and select Center for Internet Security (CIS) AWS Foundations Benchmark to enable the standard.**
    
    AWS Security Hub provides you with a comprehensive view of your security state in AWS and helps you assess your AWS environment against security industry standards and best practices.
    
    To help you manage the security state of your organization, Security Hub supports multiple security standards. This includes the AWS Foundational Security Best Practices (FSBP) standard developed by AWS, as well as external compliance frameworks. These include the Center for Internet Security (CIS), the Payment Card Industry Data Security Standard (PCI DSS), and the National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST). Each standard includes several security controls, each of which represents a security best practice. Security Hub runs checks against security controls and generates control findings to help you assess your compliance against security best practices.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Enabling_Security_Hub_16082023.png)
    
    AWS Config provides a detailed view of the configuration of AWS resources in your AWS account. This includes how the resources are related to one another and how they were configured in the past so that you can see how the configurations and relationships change over time.
    
    AWS Security Hub uses service-linked AWS Config rules to perform most of its security checks for controls. To support these controls, AWS Config must be enabled on all accounts—both the administrator account and member accounts—in each AWS Region where Security Hub is enabled. At a minimum, AWS Config must be configured to record resources that are required for the controls that you have enabled in each enabled standard.
    
    Security Hub recommends that you enable resource recording in AWS Config before you enable Security Hub standards. If Security Hub tries to run security checks when resource recording is not enabled, the checks return errors.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Turn on AWS Config and configure it to record all the resources in all regions and global resources. Enable AWS Security Hub and select Center for Internet Security (CIS) AWS Foundations Benchmark to enable the standard.**
    
    The option that says: **Turn on AWS Config and configure it to record all the resources in all regions and global resources. Deploy Operational Best Practices for CIS AWS Foundations Benchmark conformance pack in AWS Config** is incorrect. Although this is a valid solution, using AWS Config alone will require more operational overhead because you would need to create custom rules in addition to the deployed conformance pack. AWS Security Hub also receives findings from other AWS services—such as Amazon GuardDuty, Amazon Inspector, and Amazon Macie, and provides a more comprehensive view of compliance assessment. Overall, AWS Security Hub, with the help of AWS Config, is a more complete and better implementation.
    
    The option that says: **Activate Amazon Inspector and configure it to scan resources in all regions. Enable AWS Security Hub and select Center for Internet Security (CIS) AWS Foundations Benchmark to enable the standard** is incorrect because even if enabling AWS Security Hub with Center for Internet Security (CIS) AWS Foundations Benchmark standard will definitely provide compliance assessments, it will still not properly function without turning on AWS Config. Amazon Inspector can help detect software vulnerabilities and network exposure; however, it is limited only to Amazon EC2 instances, container images in Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR), and AWS Lambda functions.
    
    The option that says: **Activate Amazon Inspector and configure it to scan resources based on the Center for Internet Security’s CIS Benchmarks in all regions. Enable AWS Security Hub and select AWS Foundational Security Best Practices (FSBP) to enable the standard** is incorrect. It is true that the Amazon Inspector can help check the configuration against the security configuration best practices developed by CIS; however, Amazon Inspector remains limited to Amazon EC2 instances only, specifically for Amazon Linux 2. It will not check the configuration for the other AWS resources. Enabling Security Hub is a step on the right track, but the security engineer should also include the Center for Internet Security (CIS) AWS Foundations Benchmark standard.
    
- Activate Amazon Inspector and configure it to scan resources in all regions. Enable AWS Security Hub and select Center for Internet Security (CIS) AWS Foundations Benchmark to enable the standard.

A company aims to use a bucket policy to restrict access to an Amazon S3 bucket named “tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket” for a particular federated user named Bill. It is important to maintain Bill’s existing permissions unchanged while applying this bucket policy exclusively to his S3 access.

Which bucket policy would BEST fulfill these requirements?

- **`{	"Version": "2012-10-17",	"Statement": [{	"Sid": "DenyFederatedUser",	"Effect": "Deny",	"Principal": {	"AWS": "arn:aws:sts::123456789012:federated-user/Bill"	},	"Action": "s3:*",	"Resource": [	"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket",	"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket/*"	]	}] }`**
    
    You can use AWS Security Token Service (AWS STS) to create and provide trusted users with temporary security credentials that can control access to your AWS resources. After AWS STS issues temporary security credentials, they are valid through the expiration period and cannot be revoked.
    
    One way of getting temporary security credentials is by calling the `GetFederationToken` API. The `GetFederationToken` operation is called by an IAM user and returns temporary credentials for that user. This operation federates the user. The permissions assigned a federated user are defined in one of two places:
    
    – The session policies passed as a parameter of the `GetFederationToken` API call. (This is most common.)
    
    – A resource-based policy that explicitly names the federated user in the Principal element of the policy. (This is less common.)
    
    Some AWS resources support resource-based policies, and these policies provide another mechanism to grant permissions directly to a federated user. Only some AWS services support resource-based policies. For example, Amazon S3 has buckets, Amazon SNS has topics, and Amazon SQS has queues which you can attach policies.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/GetFederatinToken_diagram_BJL_26082023.png)
    
    Even though the session policies are not passed to the `GetFederationToken` API call while allowed by the resource-based policies, the federated user will still be able to access the resource. The same thing with the deny; even though the session policies passed to the API call allow access to the resources, while the resource-based policies deny it, the federated user won’t be able to access the resource. The resource-based policy will take precedence.
    
    Hence, the correct S3 Bucket Policy is:
    
    ```
       {
    	"Version": "2012-10-17",
    	"Statement": [{
    		"Sid": "DenyFederatedUser",
    		"Effect": "Deny",
    		"Principal": {
    			"AWS": "arn:aws:sts::123456789012:federated-user/Bill"
    		},
    		"Action": "s3:*",
    		"Resource": [
    			"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket",
    			"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket/*"
    		]
    	}]
        }
    ```
    
    The option with an IAM Policy below is incorrect. Although this will deny federated user Bill from accessing the bucket, it will also allow access to all users from the account, which is undesirable because it is too open. This policy might give access to unintended users.
    
    ```
    {
    	"Version": "2012-10-17",
    	"Statement": [{
    		"Sid": "DenyFederatedUser",
    		"Effect": "Allow",
    		"Principal": {
    			"AWS": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:root"
    		},
    		"Action": "s3:*",
    		"Resource": [
    			"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket",
    			"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket/*"
    		],
    		"Condition": {
    			"ArnNotLike": {
    				"aws:PrincipalArn": [
    					"arn:aws:sts::123456789012:federated-user/Bill"
    				]
    			}
                    }
            }]
        }}
    ```
    
    The option with an IAM Policy below is incorrect because Bill is a federated user and does not assume roles. This policy can deny access to the user regardless of what role he assumes. However, for this scenario, Bill uses federated access, and the temporary credentials come from `GetFederationToken` API call, NOT from assuming a role.
    
    ```
    {
    	"Version": "2012-10-17",
    	"Statement": [{
    		"Sid": "DenyFederatedUser",
    		"Effect": "Deny",
    		"Principal": {
    			"AWS": "arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/*/Bill"
    		},
    		"Action": "s3:*",
    		"Resource": [
    			"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket",
    			"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket/*"
    		]
    
            }]
    
        }
    ```
    
    The IAM Policy below is incorrect because, just as mentioned in the previous option, Bill is not an IAM user but a federated user who’s getting temporary credentials from a `GetFederationToken`. You have to use the “arn:aws:sts” principal instead of the “arn:aws:iam” since federated users utilizes STS.
    
    ```
       {
    	"Version": "2012-10-17",
    	"Statement": [{
    		"Sid": "DenyFederatedUser",
    		"Effect": "Deny",
    		"Principal": {
    			"AWS": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:user/Bill"
    		},
    		"Action": "s3:*",
    		"Resource": [
    			"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket",
    			"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket/*"
    		]
    
             }]
    
        }
    ```
    
- `{	"Version": "2012-10-17",	"Statement": [{	"Sid": "DenyFederatedUser",	"Effect": "Allow",	"Principal": {	"AWS": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:root"	},	"Action": "s3:*",	"Resource": [	"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket",	"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket/*"	],	"Condition": {	"ArnNotLike": {	"aws:PrincipalArn": [	"arn:aws:sts::123456789012:federated-user/Bill"	]	} } }] }`
- `{	"Version": "2012-10-17",	"Statement": [{	"Sid": "DenyFederatedUser",	"Effect": "Deny",	"Principal": {	"AWS": "arn:aws:sts::123456789012:assumed-role/*/Bill"	},	"Action": "s3:*",	"Resource": [	"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket",	"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket/*"	] }] }`
- `{	"Version": "2012-10-17",	"Statement": [{	"Sid": "DenyFederatedUser",	"Effect": "Deny",	"Principal": {	"AWS": "arn:aws:iam::123456789012:user/Bill"	},	"Action": "s3:*",	"Resource": [	"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket",	"arn:aws:s3:::tutorialsdojo-sensitive-bucket/*"	] }] }`

A Security Administrator is setting up a logging system that will track all the changes made to the cloud resources in all AWS Regions. Any configuration made in Amazon CloudFront, Amazon Route 53, and other global AWS services should also be tracked. The solution must provide an event history of all API calls made in AWS CLI or in the AWS Management Console.

Which of the following actions should the Administrator do in this scenario?

- Create a new trail in AWS CloudTrail using the AWS CLI and pass both the `-is-multi-region-trail` and `-no-include-global-service-events` parameters. Encrypt the log files using AWS CloudHSM.
- Create a new trail in Amazon EventBridge using the AWS CLI and pass both the `-is-multi-region-trail` and `-include-global-service-events` parameters. Encrypt the log files using KMS encryption.
- **Create a new trail in AWS CloudTrail using the AWS CLI and pass both the `-is-multi-region-trail` and `-include-global-service-events` parameters. Encrypt the log files using KMS encryption.**
    
    An event in CloudTrail is the record of an activity in an AWS account. This activity can be an action taken by a user, role, or service that is monitorable by CloudTrail. CloudTrail events provide a history of both API and non-API account activity made through the AWS Management Console, AWS SDKs, command line tools, and other AWS services. There are two types of events that can be logged in CloudTrail: management events and data events. By default, trails log management events, but not data events.
    
    ![CloudTrail in all region](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/cloudtrail_all_regions_main_screen_turn_on_1_3SEP2023.png)
    
    A trail can be applied to all regions or a single region. As a best practice, create a trail that applies to all regions in the AWS partition in which you are working. This is the default setting when you create a trail in the CloudTrail console.
    
    For most services, events are recorded in the region where the action occurred. For global services such as AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM), AWS STS, Amazon CloudFront, and Route 53, events are delivered to any trail that includes global services, and are logged as occurring in US East (N. Virginia) Region.
    
    In this scenario, the company requires a logging solution that will track all of the activities of all AWS resources on all AWS Regions. CloudTrail can be used for this case with multi-region trail enabled. However, it will only cover the activities of the regional services (EC2, S3, RDS, etc.) and not for global services such as IAM, CloudFront, AWS WAF, and Route 53. In order to satisfy the requirement, you have to add the `--include-global-service-events` parameter in your AWS CLI command.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a new trail in AWS CloudTrail using the AWS CLI and pass both the `--is-multi-region-trail` and `--include-global-service-events` parameters. Encrypt the log files using KMS encryption.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a new trail in Amazon EventBridge using the AWS CLI and pass both the `--is-multi-region-trail` and `--include-global-service-events` parameters. Encrypt the log files using KMS encryption** is incorrect because EventBridge is primarily designed for event-driven architectures and integrates with CloudTrail but does not directly log API activity or resource changes.
    
    The option that says: **Create a new trail in Amazon CloudWatch Logs using the AWS CLI and pass the `--is-multi-region-trail` parameter. Encrypt the log files using AWS Secrets Manager** is incorrect because CloudWatch Logs is typically used for monitoring and log storage, not for creating trails to track API calls or resource changes. CloudTrail is specifically designed for this purpose. Additionally, AWS Secrets Manager is not used for encrypting logs; KMS is the correct encryption service.
    
    The option that says: **Create a new trail in AWS CloudTrail using the AWS CLI and pass both the `--is-multi-region-trail` and `--no-include-global-service-events` parameters. Encrypt the log files using AWS CloudHSM** is incorrect because the `--is-multi-region-trail` is not enough as you also need to add the `--include-global-service-events` parameter and not `--no-include-global-service-events`.
    
- Create a new trail in Amazon CloudWatch Logs using the AWS CLI and pass the `-is-multi-region-trail` parameter. Encrypt the log files using AWS Secrets Manager.

A leading financial firm recently migrated its applications from its on-premises network to AWS. The IT Security team needs to implement a threat detection system that continuously monitors for malicious activity and unauthorized behavior to protect the firm’s AWS accounts and workloads.

Which is the MOST suitable solution in this scenario?

- Use Amazon Macie
- **Use Amazon GuardDuty**
    
    Amazon GuardDuty is a threat detection service that continuously monitors malicious activity and unauthorized behavior to protect your AWS accounts and workloads. With the cloud, the collection and aggregation of account and network activities are simplified, but it can be time-consuming for security teams to continuously analyze event log data for potential threats. With GuardDuty, you now have an intelligent and cost-effective option for continuous threat detection in the AWS Cloud.
    
    ![Amazon GuardDuty](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/product-page-diagram-Amazon-GuardDuty_how-it-works_2AUG2023.png)
    
    This service uses machine learning, anomaly detection, and integrated threat intelligence to identify and prioritize potential threats. GuardDuty analyzes tens of billions of events across multiple AWS data sources, such as AWS CloudTrail, Amazon VPC Flow Logs, and DNS logs. With a few clicks in the AWS Management Console, GuardDuty can be enabled with no software or hardware to deploy or maintain. By integrating with Amazon EventBridge, GuardDuty alerts are actionable, easy to aggregate across multiple accounts, and straightforward to push into existing event management and workflow systems.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use Amazon GuardDuty**.
    
    **Use Amazon Macie** is incorrect because this is just a security service that uses machine learning to automatically discover, classify, and protect sensitive data in AWS.
    
    **Use AWS Shield** is incorrect because this is simply a managed Distributed Denial of Service (DDoS) protection service that safeguards applications running on AWS.
    
    **Use AWS WAF** is incorrect because this is basically a web application firewall that helps protect your web applications from common web exploits that could affect application availability, compromise security, or consume excessive resources.
    
- Use AWS Shield
- Use AWS WAF

A company has recently adopted the use of AWS Organizations to effectively manage AWS accounts for development and production environments. The Security Engineer needs to ensure that IAM users within the production accounts are prohibited from manually modifying or updating the encryption keys in AWS KMS.

Which of the following options should the Security Engineer implement to meet the above requirement?

- **Create a Service Control Policy that explicitly denies access to the specified services, and then attach it to the production Organizational Unit.**
    
    **AWS Organizations** is an account management service that enables you to consolidate multiple AWS accounts into an organization that you create and centrally manage. It includes account management and consolidated billing capabilities that enable you to better meet the budgetary, security, and compliance needs of your business. As an administrator of an organization, you can create accounts in your organization and invite existing accounts to join the organization.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws-organizations-scp-demo.jpg)
    
    In AWS Organizations, Service Control Policies (SCPs) provide centralized control over the permissions and access rights within an organization’s AWS accounts. SCPs allow you to define fine-grained permissions and restrictions that apply to all accounts or specific organizational units (OUs) within the organization.
    
    SCPs have a “deny” behavior by default, which means they deny access unless explicitly allowed. This gives the organization a powerful mechanism for controlling and restricting access across multiple AWS accounts.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a Service Control Policy that explicitly denies access to the specified services, and then attach it to the production Organizational Unit.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a Service Control Policy that explicitly denies access to the specified services, and then attach it to the root Organizational Unit** is incorrect because if you apply it to the root OU, the SCP will apply to all AWS accounts, including any OUs and accounts nested within the root OU. You should just attach the SCP to the production OU to restrict access to AWS KMS.
    
    The option that says: **Create an IAM Policy that explicitly denies access to the specified services, and associate it with an IAM group. Add both IAM and root users to the group** is incorrect because enforcing a policy on the AWS root user account is not possible. Although IAM policies are effective in managing access permissions for IAM users and groups, they do not impact the root user.
    
    The option that says: **Create an IAM Policy that explicitly denies access to the specified services, and set up AWS Config managed rule to check that all users have this policy** is incorrect because AWS Config does not have a built-in or managed rule to verify the association of users with specific IAM policies.
    
- Create an IAM Policy that explicitly denies access to the specified services, and set up AWS Config managed rule to check that all users have this policy.
- Create an IAM Policy that explicitly denies access to the specified services, and associate it with an IAM group. Add both IAM and root users to the group.
- Create a Service Control Policy that explicitly denies access to the specified services, and then attach it to the root Organizational Unit.

A company plans to use AWS Key Management Service (KMS) to encrypt confidential documents owned by different organizations. The Security team must have full control over how the KMS keys are used, however, they don’t want the operational overhead of rotating keys annually.

Which AWS KMS key type should the team use to satisfy this requirement?

- AWS managed key
- **Symmetric customer managed key**
    
    Cryptographic best practices discourage extensive reuse of encryption keys. To create new cryptographic material for your AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) KMS keys, you can create a new KMS key and then change your applications or aliases to use the new KMS key. Or, you can enable automatic key rotation for an existing customer-managed CMK.
    
    When you enable automatic key rotation for a customer-managed key, AWS KMS generates new cryptographic material for the key every year. AWS KMS also saves the KMS key’s older cryptographic material in perpetuity so it can be used to decrypt data that it encrypted. AWS KMS does not delete any rotated key material until you delete the CMK. Key rotation changes only the CMK’s backing key, which is the cryptographic material that is used in encryption operations. The CMK is the same logical resource, regardless of whether or how many times its backing key changes.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/key-rotation-auto_7SEP2023.png)
    
    Key rotation in AWS KMS is a cryptographic best practice that is designed to be transparent and easy to use. AWS KMS supports optional automatic key rotation only for customer-managed keys.
    
    - **Backing key management.** AWS KMS retains all backing keys for a KMS key, even if key rotation is disabled. The backing keys are deleted only when the KMS key is deleted. When you use a KMS key to encrypt, AWS KMS uses the current backing key. When you use the KMS key to decrypt, AWS KMS uses the backing key that was used to encrypt.
    - **Enable and disable key rotation.** Automatic key rotation is disabled by default on customer-managed KMS keys. When you enable (or re-enable) key rotation, AWS KMS automatically rotates the key 365 days after the enable date and every 365 days thereafter.
    - **Disabled KMS keys.** While a KMS key is disabled, AWS KMS does not rotate it. However, the key rotation status does not change, and you cannot change it while the key is disabled. When the key is re-enabled, if the backing key is more than 365 days old, AWS KMS rotates it immediately and every 365 days thereafter. If the backing key is less than 365 days old, AWS KMS resumes the original key rotation schedule.
    - **KMS keys pending deletion.** While a KMS key is pending deletion, AWS KMS does not rotate it. The key rotation status is set to false, and you cannot change it while deletion is pending. If deletion is canceled, the previous key rotation status is restored. If the backing key is more than 365 days old, AWS KMS rotates it immediately and every 365 days thereafter. If the backing key is less than 365 days old, AWS KMS resumes the original key rotation schedule.
    - **AWS managed KMS keys.** AWS KMS automatically rotates AWS managed keys every year (approximately 365 days). You cannot enable or disable key rotation for AWS managed keys.
    - **AWS owned KMS keys.** You cannot manage key rotation for AWS owned KMS keys. The key rotation strategy for an AWS owned CMK is determined by the AWS service that creates and manages the KMS key.
    - **Monitoring key rotation.** When AWS KMS automatically rotates the key material for an AWS managed key or customer-managed key, it writes the KMS key Rotation event to Amazon CloudWatch Events. You can use this event to verify that the key was rotated.
    - **Unsupported KMS key types.** Automatic key rotation is not supported on the following types of KMS keys, but you can rotate these keys manually.
    
    1. Asymmetric KMS keys
    
    2. KMS keys in custom key stores
    
    3. KMS keys that have imported key material
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Symmetric customer managed key.**
    
    The option that says: **AWS managed key** is incorrect because key policies for AWS managed keys, which are needed to control access to KMS keys, cannot be modified.
    
    The option that says: **Customer managed key in custom key store associated with an AWS CloudHSM cluster in your account** is incorrect because automatic key rotation is not supported for this KMS key type.
    
    The option that says: **AWS owned key** is incorrect because you cannot control the level of access for AWS owned KMS key. Also, the key rotation period for an AWS owned key is determined by the AWS service that creates and manages the KMS key. Its automatic key rotation varies depending on the AWS service.
    
- Customer managed key in a custom key store associated with an AWS CloudHSM cluster in your account
- AWS owned key

A company plans to use a Network Load Balancer (NLB) to distribute traffic among a group of EC2 instances in an Amazon VPC. However, to ensure the security of their VPC, a security team must be able to inspect traffic that enters and exits the VPC using an intrusion detection system (IDS).

Which approach satisfies the requirements?

- Enable Traffic Mirroring on the NLB and forward traffic to the instances. Create a traffic mirror filter to inspect the ingress and egress of data that traverses the VPC.
- **Create a firewall using AWS Network Firewall at the VPC level, then add custom rule groups to inspect ingress and egress traffic. Update the necessary VPC route tables.**
    
    AWS Network Firewall is a stateful, managed, network firewall, and intrusion detection and prevention service for your virtual private cloud (VPC).
