With Network Firewall, you can filter traffic at the perimeter of your VPC. This includes traffic going to and coming from an internet gateway, NAT gateway, or over VPN or AWS Direct Connect. Network Firewall uses Suricata — an open-source intrusion prevention system (IPS) for stateful inspection.
    
    The diagram below shows an AWS Network firewall deployed in a single availability zone and traffic flow for a workload in a public subnet:
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws-network-firewall-single-az-deployment-diagram.jpg)
    
    You can use Network Firewall to monitor and protect your Amazon VPC traffic in a number of ways, including the following:
    
    – Pass traffic through only from known AWS service domains or IP address endpoints, such as Amazon S3.
    
    – Use custom lists of known bad domains to limit the types of domain names that your applications can access.
    
    – Perform deep packet inspection on traffic entering or leaving your VPC.
    
    – Use stateful protocol detection to filter protocols like HTTPS, independent of the port used.
    
    Therefore, the correct answer is: **Create a firewall using AWS Network Firewall at the VPC level, then add custom rule groups to inspect ingress and egress traffic. Update the necessary VPC route tables.**
    
    The option that says: **Inspect ingress and egress traffic of the VPC using Network Access Analyzer** is incorrect. Network Access Analyzer is a VPC feature that reports on unintended access to your AWS resources based on the security and compliance that you set. This service is not capable of performing deep packet inspection on traffic entering or leaving your VPC, unlike AWS Network Firewall.
    
    The option that says: **Create a firewall at the subnet level using Amazon Detective and inspect the ingress and egress traffic using the VPC Reachability Analyzer** is incorrect because a firewall must be created at the VPC level and not at the subnet level. Moreover, Amazon Detective can’t be used to create a firewall. This service just automatically collects log data from your AWS resources to analyze, investigate, and quickly identify the root cause of potential security issues or suspicious activities in your AWS account. For this scenario, you have to use the AWS Network Firewall instead.
    
    The option that says: **Enable Traffic Mirroring on the NLB and forward traffic to the instances. Create a traffic mirror filter to inspect the ingress and egress of data that traverses the VPC** is incorrect, as this alone accomplishes nothing. It would make more sense to redirect the traffic to an EC2 instance where an IDS is running. Remember that Traffic Mirroring is simply an Amazon VPC feature that you can use to copy network traffic from an elastic network interface. Traffic mirror filters can’t inspect the actual packet of the incoming and outgoing traffic.
    
- Create a firewall at the subnet level using Amazon Detective and inspect the ingress and egress traffic using the VPC Reachability Analyzer.
- Inspect ingress and egress traffic of the VPC using Network Access Analyzer.

Due to a recent security incident, one of the company’s EC2 key pairs in the Ohio region was compromised. A security admin has been tasked with figuring out which EC2 instances were launched using the compromised key pair.

What should the security admin do?

- Log in to the Amazon EC2 Console and get the fingerprint of the key pair. Contact AWS Support and send them the fingerprint.
- Use the `aws ec2 describe-key-pairs --filters "Name=key-name,Values=NameOfKeyPair"` command to retrieve the key pair’s fingerprint. Contact AWS Support and send them the fingerprint.
- **Use the `aws ec2 describe-instances --filters "Name=key-name,Values=NameOfKeyPair"` command to retrieve the list of affected instances.**
    
    The **`describe-instances`** command describes the specified instances or all instances. If you specify instance IDs, the output includes information for only the specified instances. If you specify filters, the output includes information for only those instances that meet the filter criteria.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/amazon-ec2-describe-instances.JPG)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use the `aws ec2 describe-instances --filters "Name=key-name,Values=NameOfKeyPair"` command to retrieve the list of affected instances.**
    
    Your key pair fingerprint can be retrieved via the EC2 Console or AWS SDK using the `aws ec2 describe-key-pairs` command so the following options are basically the same thing. You don’t have to know the specific fingerprint of a key pair to identify the EC2 instance using it. Hence, these options are incorrect:
    
    **– Use the `aws ec2 describe-key-pairs --filters "Name=key-name,Values=NameOfKeyPair"` command to retrieve the key pair’s fingerprint. Contact AWS Support and send them the fingerprint.**
    
    **– Log in to the Amazon EC2 Console and get the fingerprint of the key pair. Contact AWS Support and send them the fingerprint.**
    
    The option that says: **SSH to one of the EC2 instances and send a GET request to `http: http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/public-keys/`** is incorrect. This option will just give you the list of available public keys in the instance you’re currently logged in.
    
- SSH to one of the EC2 instances and send a GET request to `http: http://169.254.169.254/latest/meta-data/public-keys/`.

A leading EdTech company wants to implement DNS Security Extensions (DNSSEC) to its subdomain that is already registered with Amazon Route 53. The security engineer has activated DNSSEC signing and generated a key-signing key (KSK).

However, when the security engineer attempts to test the configuration, she encounters an error indicating a broken trust chain, as shown below:

`error (broken trust chain) resolving 'manila.tutorialsdojo.com/DS/IN'`

Which of the following solution should the security engineer implement to resolve the error?

- Disable and subsequently enable the key-signing key (KSK).
- **Generate a Delegation Signer (DS) record in the parent-hosted zone.**
    
    **Domain Name System Security Extensions (DNSSEC)** signing lets DNS resolvers validate that a DNS response came from Amazon Route 53 and has not been tampered with. When you use DNSSEC signing, every response for a hosted zone is signed using public key cryptography.
    
    Attackers sometimes hijack traffic to internet endpoints such as web servers by intercepting DNS queries and returning their own IP addresses to DNS resolvers in place of the actual IP addresses for those endpoints. Users are then routed to the IP addresses provided by the attackers in the spoofed response, for example, to fake websites.
    
    You can protect your domain from this type of attack, known as DNS spoofing or a man-in-the-middle attack, by configuring Domain Name System Security Extensions (DNSSEC), a protocol for securing DNS traffic.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/Route53_DNSSEC.png)
    
    There are two kinds of keys in DNSSEC:
    
    – key-signing key (KSK)
    
    – zone-signing key (ZSK)
    
    In Route 53 DNSSEC signing, each KSK is based on an asymmetric customer managed key in AWS KMS. You are responsible for KSK management, which includes rotating it if needed. ZSK management is performed by Route 53.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/scs-c02-dnssec.png)
    
    To enable DNSSEC signing and establishing a chain of trust:
    
    **Step 1:** Prepare for enabling DNSSEC signing.
    
    **Step 2:** Enable DNSSEC signing and create a KSK.
    
    **Step 3:** Establish a chain of trust.
    
    After enabling DNSSEC signing for a hosted zone in Route 53, establish a chain of trust for the hosted zone to complete your DNSSEC signing setup. You do this by **creating a Delegation Signer (DS) record** **in the parent hosted zone**, for your hosted zone, using the information that Route 53 provides. Depending on where your domain is registered, you add the record to the parent-hosted zone in Route 53 or at another domain registrar.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Generate a Delegation Signer (DS) record in the parent-hosted zone.**
    
    The option that says: **Substitute the key-signing key (KSK) with a zone-signing key (ZSK)** is incorrect because only replacing the KSK with a ZSK would not resolve any trust chain issues. In fact, it would likely introduce security vulnerabilities. The KSK is a critical component of DNSSEC’s security, and it serves as the ultimate trust anchor for validating DNS data. Without a valid and secure KSK, the entire DNSSEC chain of trust would be compromised.
    
    The option that says: **Disable and subsequently enable the key-signing key (KSK)** is incorrect because the trust chain relies on the proper configuration and maintenance of KSKs and Zone-Signing Keys (ZSKs). Simply disabling and then re-enabling the KSK will not resolve the issue.
    
    The option that says: **Create a Delegation Signer (DS) record in the subdomain** is incorrect because Amazon Route 53 does not support the direct creation of Delegation Signer (DS) records within a subdomain. Delegation Signer (DS) records are typically managed at the parent zone level, where the parent zone’s DNSSEC configuration includes DS records that correspond to the child (subdomain) zone’s KSK (Key Signing Key).
    
- Substitute the key-signing key (KSK) with a zone-signing key (ZSK).
- Create a Delegation Signer (DS) record in the subdomain.

A company has experienced an OS boot issue on one of its Windows servers hosted on an EC2 instance. The company’s security engineer hopes to diagnose the problem by analyzing the memory dump files persisting on the instance.

Which action should the engineer do to collect the instance’s memory dump?

- **Download the EC2Rescue tool for Windows Server on the EC2 instance. Manually run it to collect memory dumps.**
    
    EC2Rescue for EC2 Windows is a troubleshooting tool that you can run on your Amazon EC2 Windows Server instances. Use the tool to troubleshoot OS-level issues and to collect advanced logs and configuration files for further analysis. The following are some common issues that EC2Rescue can address:
    
    – *Instance connectivity issues due to firewall, Remote Desktop Protocol (RDP), or network interface configuration.*
    
    – *OS boot issues due to a blue screen or stop error, a boot loop, or a corrupted registry.*
    
    – *Any issues that might need advanced log analysis and troubleshooting*.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-SSM-EC2Rescue.png)
    
    You can run EC2Rescue manually or automatically using the AWS Systems Manager `AWSSupport-ExecuteEC2Res` runbook.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Download the EC2Rescue tool for Windows Server on the EC2 instance. Manually run it to collect memory dumps.**
    
    The option that says: **Install the Amazon Inspector agent on the EC2 instance and run a host assessment to collect the memory dumps** is incorrect. Amazon Inspector does not have this capability. A host assessment just checks for common vulnerabilities and exposures, CIS benchmarks, and security best practices.
    
    The option that says: **Install the AWS Systems Manager (SSM) agent on the EC2 instance and execute the `AWS-ConfigureCloudWatchOnEC2Instance` runbook to allow SSM to publish the memory dumps to CloudWatch Logs** is incorrect. This runbook is just used for enabling or disabling Amazon CloudWatch monitoring on managed instances and will not perform memory dump collection.
    
    The option that says: **Capture and download a screenshot of the affected instance from the EC2 Console to review the memory dumps** is incorrect. An EC2 instance screenshot gives additional visibility to the current state of your instance. This is useful for troubleshooting but it does contain the memory dump files required in the scenario.
    
- Capture and download a screenshot of the affected instance from the EC2 Console to review the memory dumps.
- Install the AWS Systems Manager (SSM) agent on the EC2 instance and execute the `AWS-ConfigureCloudWatchOnEC2Instance` runbook to allow SSM to publish the memory dumps to CloudWatch Logs.
- Install the Amazon Inspector agent on the EC2 instance and run a host assessment to collect the memory dumps.

A company has an existing Microsoft Active Directory on-premises for user authentication. The company is already using AWS Organizations to manage multiple AWS Accounts and wants to use the existing Active Directory on-premises for authenticating the users and applications. An AWS Direct Connect connection is in place between on-premises and all the AWS accounts.

The company’s security policy mandates that access to accounts be constrained based on the user’s membership in particular groups and assigned roles. The management of user identities must take place in a centralized location.

Which solution will meet these requirements?

- Set up Amazon Cognito to connect to Active Directory using SAML 2.0. Use the System for Cross-domain Identity Management (SCIM) v2.0 protocol to enable automatic provisioning. Grant access to the AWS accounts by using Cognito user pools.
- Set up AWS Directory Service for Microsoft Active Directory (AWS Managed Microsoft AD) as the identity source. Configure trust relationships between AWS Managed Microsoft AD and AWS accounts. Use AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) to manage user access and permissions.
- Set up an IAM OIDC identity provider to connect with Active Directory. Use IAM roles with OIDC claims-based access control to grant users access to AWS accounts based on their Active Directory group memberships.
- **Set up AWS IAM Identity Center to use SAML 2.0 to connect to Active Directory. Use the System for Cross-domain Identity Management (SCIM) v2.0 protocol to enable automatic provisioning. Use attribute-based access controls (ABACs) to grant access to the AWS accounts.**
    
    With AWS IAM Identity Center (successor to AWS Single Sign-On), you can manage sign-in security for your workforce identities, also known as workforce users. IAM Identity Center provides one place where you can create or connect workforce users and centrally manage their access across all their AWS accounts and applications.
    
    Both SCIM and SAML are important considerations for configuring IAM Identity Center.
    
    You can use AWS IAM Identity Center (successor to AWS Single Sign-On) to authenticate identities from the IdPs through the Security Assertion Markup Language (SAML) 2.0 standard. This enables your users to sign in to the AWS access portal with their corporate credentials. They can then navigate to their assigned accounts, roles, and applications hosted in external IdPs.
    
    IAM Identity Center supports automatic provisioning (synchronization) of user and group information from your identity provider (IdP) into IAM Identity Center using the System for Cross-domain Identity Management (SCIM) v2.0 protocol. When you configure SCIM synchronization, you create a mapping of your identity provider (IdP) user attributes to the named attributes in IAM Identity Center. This causes the expected attributes to match between IAM Identity Center and your IdP.
    
    ![Configuring external identity provider](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/identity_center_extaernal_idP_BJL_24062023.png)
    
    To allow users to use their Active Directory credentials in accessing AWS Accounts within AWS Organizations, you should establish a trust relationship between your on-premises Active Directory and AWS by setting up AWS IAM Identity Center with SAML 2.0.
    
    To synchronize user attributes, such as username, email address, or group membership, enable System for Cross-domain Identity Management (SCIM) v2.0 automatic provisioning, which allows the management of user identities in a centralized location. To ensure access to accounts is constrained based on the user’s membership in particular groups and assigned roles AWS IAM Identity Center provides attribute-based access controls (ABACs), which let you define policies that grant access to AWS Accounts based on attributes like group membership or assigned roles in the Active Directory.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up AWS IAM Identity Center to use SAML 2.0 to connect to Active Directory. Use the System for Cross-domain Identity Management (SCIM) v2.0 protocol to enable automatic provisioning. Use attribute-based access controls (ABACs) to grant access to the AWS accounts.**
    
    The option that says: **Set up Amazon Cognito to connect to Active Directory using SAML 2.0. Use the System for Cross-domain Identity Management (SCIM) v2.0 protocol to enable automatic provisioning. Grant access to the AWS accounts by using Cognito user pools** is incorrect because while Amazon Cognito supports SAML 2.0 integration with Active Directory, it is only intended for user authentication and authorization for mobile and web applications rather than controlling access to AWS accounts within AWS Organizations. It can be an alternative solution when you want to authenticate users using their Active Directory credentials, but it will not provide the same level of centralized control as AWS IAM Identity Center in terms of user management and access control across AWS Organizations.
    
    The option that says: **Set up an IAM OIDC identity provider to connect with Active Directory. Use IAM roles with OIDC claims-based access control to grant users access to AWS accounts based on their Active Directory group memberships** is incorrect because OIDC (OpenID Connect) is not natively supported by Microsoft Active Directory for authentication purposes. Active Directory primarily uses SAML for federated authentication. AWS IAM OIDC identity providers are commonly used for integrating third-party authentication services such as Google or Salesforce, but they do not support direct federation with on-prem Active Directory. Additionally, IAM roles do not have built-in OIDC group-based access control for AWS Organizations.
    
    The option that says: **Set up AWS Directory Service for Microsoft Active Directory (AWS Managed Microsoft AD) as the identity source. Configure trust relationships between AWS Managed Microsoft AD and AWS accounts. Use AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) to manage user access and permissions** is incorrect. Although this is a viable solution, it can integrate with on-premises Active Directory and provide access controls based on groups and roles. However, aside from a lack of direct integration with AWS Organizations, it will also have duplication of identity management since it introduces a separate identity management system alongside the existing on-premises Active Directory. This duplication of user accounts and management systems can lead to inconsistencies and difficulties in maintaining a centralized view of user identities and access control.
    

A company is overseeing several AWS accounts through AWS Organizations and AWS Control Tower. There is an Amazon S3 bucket that acts as a centralized log repository for all of the AWS CloudTrail logs of every member account.

The security engineer observed that certain member accounts are not sending CloudTrail logs to the centralized S3 bucket. She wants to guarantee that the existing and future AWS accounts will have at least one CloudTrail trail configured.

What actions should the security engineer take to satisfy this requirement?

- Set up a Service Control Policy (SCP) that denies `cloudtrail:Delete*`and `cloudtrail:Stop*` actions. Apply the SCP to all member accounts.
- **Update the trail in the master account’s AWS CloudTrail to enable the logging for all member accounts and apply it to the AWS organization.**
    
    If you have created an organization in AWS Organizations, you can create a trail that logs all events for all AWS accounts in that organization. You can choose to edit an existing trail in the management account or delegated administrator account and apply it to an organization, making it an organization trail. Organization trails log events for the management account, and all member accounts in the organization.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Organization_Trail_AWS_Doc_14082023.png)
    
    When you create an organization trail, a trail with the name that you give it is created in every AWS account that belongs to your organization. Users with CloudTrail permissions in member accounts can see this trail when they log into the AWS CloudTrail console from their AWS accounts, or when they run AWS CLI commands such as describe-trail. However, users in member accounts do not have sufficient permissions to delete the organization trail, turn logging on or off, change what types of events are logged, or otherwise change the organization trail in any way.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws_orgazniations_cloud_trail_centralized_tutorialsdojo.png)
    
    The trail logs activity for all accounts in the organization in the same Amazon S3 bucket. Only the management account or delegated administrator account can change or delete the trail for the organization. Only the management account can remove a member account from an organization. Similarly, by default, only the management account has access to the Amazon S3 bucket for the trail and the logs contained within it.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Update the trail in the master account’s AWS CloudTrail to enable the logging for all member accounts and apply it to the AWS organization.**
    
    The option that says: **Set up a Service Control Policy (SCP) `cloudtrail:Delete*` and `cloudtrail:Stop*` actions. Apply the SCP to all member accounts** is incorrect. Even though this will help ensure that member accounts cannot stop trail logging or delete trails, it does not mean that a member account’s CloudTrail trail will exist and log to a centralized bucket. This SCP policy can also be too powerful, as adding an asterisk(*) will deny all delete and stop actions for CloudTrail. This implementation is unnecessary because once a CloudTrail trail is configured from the management account, it will ensure that all the member accounts cannot modify, delete or stop the CloudTrail trail and the centralized S3 bucket.
    
    The option that says: **Create a new trail on all member accounts and configure it to send the CloudTrail logs to the centralized S3 bucket. Set up an Amazon EventBridge rule that automatically sends a notification if the trail is either deleted or stopped** is incorrect. This can help ensure a CloudTrail trail exists and will always log the API calls; however, this will make the management complex for future member accounts. You will have to implement this every time a new member account is added to the organization.
    
    The option that says: **Create a `cloudtrail-enabled` managed rule in AWS Config. Set up an Amazon EventBridge rule that sends a notification when non-compliance is detected from the AWS Config rule** is incorrect. Although it helps to notify the team whether the CloudTrail trail exists or logs, it does not give any action that will ensure it stays that way. It will help in monitoring the status of the CloudTrail trail but does not satisfy the requirement of ensuring that the existing and future AWS accounts will have at least one CloudTrail trail configured.
    
- Create a new trail on all member accounts and configure it to send the CloudTrail logs to the centralized S3 bucket. Set up an Amazon EventBridge rule that automatically sends a notification if the trail is either deleted or stopped.
- Create a `cloudtrail-enabled` managed rule in AWS Config. Set up an Amazon EventBridge rule that sends a notification when non-compliance is detected from the AWS Config rule.

A company hosting a website in the AWS aims to strengthen its security measures. The company’s security team has already implemented AWS Security Hub to gain a more comprehensive view of its security posture.

Additionally, they want to safeguard the website against potential threats such as man-in-the-middle attacks using Amazon CloudFront. The company has an existing CloudFront distribution with an associated SimpleCORS managed policy.

Which approach would best fulfill these criteria with the LEAST operational overhead?

- Use the CORS-With-Preflight managed policy to add a set of security headers to all responses that CloudFront sends to viewers.
- **Use the SecurityHeadersPolicy managed policy to add a set of security headers to all responses that CloudFront sends to viewers.**
    
    Amazon CloudFront is a web service that speeds up the distribution of your static and dynamic web content, such as .html, .css, .js, and image files, to your users. CloudFront delivers your content through a worldwide network of data centers called edge locations. When a user requests content that you’re serving with CloudFront, the request is routed to the edge location that provides the lowest latency (time delay) so that content is delivered with the best possible performance.
    
    Amazon CloudFront offers three different kinds of policies that you can use to customize CloudFront in the following ways:
    
    – Specify cache and compression settings
    
    – Specify values to include in origin requests (but not in the cache key)
    
    – Specify HTTP headers to remove or add in viewer responses
    
    With a CloudFront response headers policy, you can control the HTTP headers that CloudFront includes in HTTP responses that it sends to viewers (web browsers or other clients). You can remove headers from the origin’s HTTP response or add HTTP headers to the responses that CloudFront sends to viewers without making any changes to your origin or writing any code.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Cloudfront_Security_Headers_BJL_30082023.png)
    
    You can use SecurityHeadersPolicy managed policy to add a set of security headers to all responses that CloudFront sends to viewers. With this response headers policy, CloudFront adds `X-Content-Type-Options: nosniff` to all responses. This is the case when the response that CloudFront received from the origin included this header and when it didn’t
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use the SecurityHeadersPolicy managed policy to add a set of security headers to all responses that CloudFront sends to viewers.**
    
    The option that says: **Use the CORS-With-Preflight managed policy to add a set of security headers to all responses that CloudFront sends to viewers** is incorrect. Even though it is true that adding HTTP headers can help mitigate potential threats like man-in-the-middle attacks, and using a managed policy with CloudFront can help you achieve that, using CORS-With-Preflight managed policy will not help you add the specific headers that you need for this type of attack. The CORS-With-Preflight managed policy allows CORS requests from any origin, including preflight requests. This configuration is primarily used for allowing a website to access intended content from other domains, not for mitigating man-in-the-middle attacks.
    
    The option that says: **Use the CloudFront functions to add a set of security headers, such as the Strict-Transport-Security response header, to all responses that CloudFront sends to viewers** is incorrect. While utilizing CloudFront functions to add security headers can help safeguard the website against potential threats such as man-in-the-middle attacks, creating a custom function and managing it would just add complexity to the solution. There is already a readily available managed response headers policy that you can utilize to add security headers from the CloudFront.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS WAF with CloudFront to control access to your content. Set up a custom response headers policy that includes the X-XSS-Protection header for each and every request** is incorrect because AWS WAF is primarily used for giving protection from the most common vulnerabilities found in web applications, as described in the Open Worldwide Application Security Project (OWASP) Top 10, and not directly used for mitigating man-in-the-middle attacks. It can definitely improve your security posture but does not directly satisfy the requirement.
    
- Use the CloudFront functions to add a set of security headers, such as the Strict-Transport-Security response header, to all responses that CloudFront sends to viewers
- Use AWS WAF with CloudFront to control access to your content. Set up a custom response headers policy that includes the X-XSS-Protection header for each and every request.

A social media company leverages multiple Amazon S3 buckets to effectively manage and accommodate the extensive volume of media content generated by its users. The IT Security team is planning to implement a solution that can detect whether a private bucket becomes accessible over the internet using Amazon GuardDuty S3 Protection.

Which of the following S3 finding types fulfills this requirement?

- `Policy:S3/BucketPublicAccessGranted`
- `Policy:S3/BucketBlockPublicAccessDisabled`
- **`Policy:S3/BucketAnonymousAccessGranted`**
    
    **Amazon GuardDuty** offers important findings specifically targeting Amazon S3, aimed at detecting and addressing security risks within S3 environments. These findings cover data exfiltration, identifying suspicious data transfers or unauthorized data movement from S3 buckets, and access control issues, alerting on misconfigurations or improper access permissions that could lead to data breaches. By leveraging GuardDuty’s S3-specific findings, organizations can proactively identify and address potential security threats, ensuring the protection of their S3 data.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws-guardduty-s3-findings.jpg)
    
    The **“Policy:S3/BucketAnonymousAccessGranted”** finding type indicates that a bucket policy or access control list (ACL) has been configured to make an Amazon S3 bucket publicly accessible on the internet. It aligns with the IT Security team’s objective to detect if a private bucket becomes accessible over the internet.
    
    By leveraging Amazon GuardDuty with S3 Protection, the team can identify any potential misconfigurations or unauthorized access to the private buckets, ensuring the security and privacy of the media content generated by the social media company’s users.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **`Policy:S3/BucketAnonymousAccessGranted`.**
    
    The option that says: **`Policy:S3/BucketPublicAccessGranted`** is incorrect because this finding type signifies that an Amazon S3 bucket has been configured to allow public access. While it is related to public access, it does not specifically address the detection of a private bucket becoming accessible over the internet. It is more relevant in scenarios where intentional public access is desired.
    
    The option that says: **`Policy:S3/AccountBlockPublicAccessDisabled`** is incorrect because this finding type indicates that the account-level settings for blocking public access in Amazon S3 have been disabled. However, in the provided scenario, the focus is on detecting whether a private bucket becomes accessible over the internet. This finding type is not directly related to identifying public access to individual buckets.
    
    The option that says: **`Policy:S3/BucketBlockPublicAccessDisabled`** is incorrect because this finding type indicates that the bucket-level settings for blocking public access in Amazon S3 have been disabled. Similar to the previous finding type, it is not focused on detecting the specific scenario of a private bucket becoming accessible over the internet.
    
- `Policy:S3/AccountBlockPublicAccessDisabled`

A financial services company is planning to deploy a mission-critical application in the AWS cloud. The Enterprise Architect has been tasked to design a secure and functional solution incorporating multiple AWS services. To further improve the security posture of the application, the workload has been added in the AWS Well-Architected Tool (WA Tool) to check against architectural best practices.

The developers must be able to establish an SSH connection to the bastion host using the corporate workstation. To enhance application security, the solution needs to apply security groups to the following resources:

- td-sgBH – attach to Linux Bastion Host.
- td-sgLB – attach to AWS Application Load Balancer.
- td-sgAPP – attach to Amazon EC2 instances.
- td-sgDB – attach to Amazon RDS for MySQL.

How should the security group be configured to meet the above requirements?

- – td-sgBH – allow port 22 traffic only from the VPC IP address range.– td-sgLB – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from any source (0.0.0.0/0).– td-sgAPP – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from td-sgLB.– td-sgDB – allow port 3306 traffic from both td-sgAPP and td-sgLB.
- **– td-sgBH – allow port 22 traffic only from the corporate IP address range.– td-sgLB – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from any source (0.0.0.0/0).– td-sgAPP – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from td-sgLB.– td-sgDB – allow port 3306 traffic from both td-sgAPP and td-sgBH.**
    
    An AWS **security group** is a fundamental component for controlling inbound and outbound traffic to your AWS resources. It acts as a virtual firewall that regulates access to your instances, allowing you to define rules for traffic flow. By properly configuring security groups, you can enhance the security posture of your AWS infrastructure, enabling secure and controlled communication between resources and protecting your data and applications from unauthorized access.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-SG-InboundRuleSSH.PNG)
    
    To ensure that the resources are secure, the security group should have the following configuration:
    
    **– td-sgBH** – allow port 22 traffic only from the corporate IP address range. This configuration enables SSH traffic from the corporate workstation, allowing administrators to connect to the host.
    
    **– td-sgLB** – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from any source (0.0.0.0/0). This configuration permits all HTTP and HTTPS traffic to the ALB, which serves as the front-facing load balancer.
    
    **– td-sgAPP** – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from td-sgLB. This configuration enables HTTP and HTTPS traffic from the ALB to reach the instances behind it.
    
    **– td-sgDB** – allow port 3306 traffic from both td-sgAPP and td-sgBH. This configuration allows TCP traffic from both the bastion host and the EC2 instances to access the RDS for MySQL DB instance. Please note that the default MYSQL port is 3306.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is:
    
    **– td-sgBH – allow port 22 traffic only from the corporate IP address range.**
    
    **– td-sgLB – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from any source (0.0.0.0/0).**
    
    **– td-sgAPP – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from td-sgLB.**
    
    **– td-sgDB – allow port 3306 traffic from both td-sgAPP and td-sgBH.**
    
    The option that says:
    
    **– td-sgBH – allow port 22 traffic only from the VPC IP address range.**
    
    **– td-sgLB – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from any source (0.0.0.0/0).**
    
    **– td-sgAPP – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from td-sgLB.**
    
    **– td-sgDB – allow port 3306 traffic from both td-sgAPP and td-sgLB.**
    
    is incorrect because the td-SGDB should only allow traffic from Amazon EC2 instances and Linux bastion host.
    
    The option that says:
    
    **– td-sgBH – allow port 22 traffic only from the corporate IP address range.**
    
    **– td-sgLB – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from any source (0.0.0.0/0).**
    
    **– td-sgAPP – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from any source (0.0.0.0/0).**
    
    **– td-sgDB – allow port 3306 traffic from both td-sgAPP and td-sgBH.**
    
    is incorrect because the td-sgAPP should only allow traffic from the AWS Application Load Balancer.
    
    The option that says:
    
    **– td-sgBH – allow port 22 traffic only from the VPC IP address range.**
    
    **– td-sgLB – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from any source (0.0.0.0/0).**
    
    **– td-sgAPP – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from td-sgLB.**
    
    **– td-sgDB – allow port 3306 traffic from both td-sgAPP and td-sgBH.**
    
    is incorrect because the td-sgBH should only allow traffic from the corporate IP address range.
    
- – td-sgBH – allow port 22 traffic only from the VPC IP address range.– td-sgLB – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from any source (0.0.0.0/0).– td-sgAPP – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from td-sgLB.– td-sgDB – allow port 3306 traffic from both td-sgAPP and td-sgBH.
- – td-sgBH – allow port 22 traffic only from the corporate IP address range.– td-sgLB – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from any source (0.0.0.0/0).– td-sgAPP – allow port 80 and 443 traffic from any source (0.0.0.0/0).td-sgDB – allow port 3306 traffic from both td-sgAPP and td-sgBH.

A company aims to implement a comprehensive security threat-detection system across all its member accounts within AWS Organizations, specifically in the us-east-1 region. The organization consists of a management account, a security account, and numerous member accounts.

Whenever a new member account is established, the threat-detection service should automatically analyze the AWS account to allow the company to assess any identified findings from the security account.

Which approach adheres to AWS security best practices and fulfills these requirements with MINIMAL complexity?

- Enable Amazon GuardDuty in us-east-1. Designate the security account as delegated administrator for Amazon GuardDuty. Create an Amazon EventBridge rule for `CreateAccount` API call to target a Lambda function that will enable the Amazon GuardDuty to the new member account.
- Enable AWS Security Hub in us-east-1. Designate the security account as delegated administrator for AWS Security Hub. Turn on automatic enablement for new member accounts.
- Enable AWS Control Tower in us-east-1. Designate the security account as delegated administrator for Amazon GuardDuty. Utilize the Account Factory Customization (AFC) feature to automatically enable Amazon GuardDuty to new member accounts.
- **Enable Amazon GuardDuty in us-east-1. Designate the security account as delegated administrator for Amazon GuardDuty. Activate auto-enable for all new member accounts.**
    
    Amazon GuardDuty is a security monitoring service that analyzes and processes Foundational data sources, such as AWS CloudTrail management events, AWS CloudTrail event logs, VPC flow logs (from Amazon EC2 instances), and DNS logs. It also processes Features such as Kubernetes audit logs, RDS login activity, S3 logs, EBS volumes, Runtime monitoring, and Lambda network activity logs. It uses threat intelligence feeds, such as lists of malicious IP addresses and domains, and machine learning to identify unexpected, potentially unauthorized, and malicious activity within your AWS environment.
    
    When you use GuardDuty with an AWS Organizations organization, you can designate any account within the organization to be the GuardDuty delegated administrator. Only the organization management account can designate a delegated administrator. Your organization’s management account can be the delegated administrator. However, the AWS security best practices follow the principle of least privilege and don’t recommend this configuration.
    
    You can configure auto-enable preferences for the organization. The Accounts page provides configuration options to the GuardDuty administrator to Auto-enable GuardDuty on behalf of the member accounts that belong to the organization. You can find this option above the section that displays the Accounts table.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Auto_enable_amazon_guardduty_BJL_29082023.png)
    
    When a delegated administrator opts out of an opt-in Region, even if your organization has the GuardDuty auto-enable configuration set to either new member accounts only (NEW) or all member accounts (ALL), GuardDuty cannot be enabled for any member account in the organization that currently has GuardDuty disabled.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is:  **Enable Amazon GuardDuty in us-east-1. Designate the security account as delegated administrator for Amazon GuardDuty. Activate auto-enable for all new member accounts.**
    
    The option that says: **Enable AWS Security Hub in us-east-1. Designate the security account as delegated administrator for AWS Security Hub. Turn on automatic enablement for new member accounts** is incorrect. Enabling AWS Security Hub can improve the security posture of your environment; however, it is not the most appropriate AWS service for threat detection and automatic analysis of the AWS account. AWS Security Hub is primarily used for providing a comprehensive view of your security posture across your AWS accounts, and it does not satisfy the requirement for the scenario. You should enable Amazon GuardDuty instead.
    
    The option that says: **Enable AWS Control Tower in us-east-1. Designate the security account as delegated administrator for Amazon GuardDuty. Utilize the Account Factory Customization (AFC) feature to automatically enable Amazon GuardDuty to new member accounts** is incorrect. Although designating the security account as the delegated administrator for Amazon GuardDuty is a step in the right direction, utilizing the AWS Control Tower and Account Factory Customization (AFC) feature is still not the BEST approach for enabling Amazon GuardDuty in the new accounts because it gives complexity to the solution and adds operational overhead. Amazon GuardDuty already has an existing feature to automatically enable Amazon GuardDuty to all new member accounts that are part of the same organization.
    
    The option that says: **Enable Amazon GuardDuty in us-east-1. Designate the security account as delegated administrator for Amazon GuardDuty. Create an Amazon EventBridge rule for `CreateAccount` API call to target a Lambda function that will enable the Amazon GuardDuty to the new member account** is incorrect. Even though this can satisfy the requirements, this is still not the BEST solution because it adds complexity and operational overhead. It is true that you can enable Amazon GuardDuty and designate a delegated administrator to implement a threat detection solution for the organization; however, creating an Amazon EventBridge and a custom AWS Lambda function is not necessary. Amazon GuardDuty already has an existing feature to automatically enable Amazon GuardDuty to all new member accounts that are part of the same organization.
    

A company owns a web application leveraging Amazon Cognito user pools for authentication. There is a reported incident involving compromised user accounts in the web application. The security engineer is tasked to investigate the incident and needs to manually verify the ID tokens and access tokens to identify any signs of unauthorized access or tampering.

What is the MOST secure method to achieve this objective

- **Use the verify method from `aws-jwt-verify` library with JSON Web token (JWT) token. Compare `sub` (User ID), `aud` (audience), `iss` (issuers claim), and exp (expiration time) from the output.**
    
    Amazon Cognito user pool is a user directory that provides sign-up and sign-in options for your app users. When a user signs into your app, Amazon Cognito verifies the login information. If the login is successful, Amazon Cognito creates a session and returns an ID, access, and refresh token for the authenticated user. You can use the tokens to grant your users access to your own server-side resources or access token. The access token is a JSON Web Token (JWT).
    
    In general, properly implemented and securely transmitted JWT tokens are resistant to tampering. However, it’s important to note that JWT tokens, like any other data transmitted over the network, can be vulnerable to tampering if appropriate security measures are not in place. This is why AWS releases `aws-jwt-verify library`, which will let you do a signature verification using the following code:
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/scs-c02_aws_cognito-aws-jwt-verify-tutorialsdojo.png)
    
    This code will also give back the claims from the payload, which can also be used to further verify the legitimacy of the token.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use the verify method from `aws-jwt-verify` library with JSON Web token (JWT) token. Compare `sub` (User ID), `aud` (audience), `iss` (issuers claim), and exp (expiration time) from the output.**
    
    The option that says: **Use `GetUser` API call with the access token to know if the token is expired. Get and compare the `client_id` claim extracted from the access token with the app client ID from the Amazon Cognito user pool** is incorrect. Although checking the token’s expiration is important, it does not address the broader concerns of unauthorized access or tampering. This method does not include signature verification, leaving the token vulnerable to manipulation.
    
    The option that says: **Get the JSON Web token (JWT) token payload. Decode JWT token using JWT Library. Extract decoded `sub` (User ID), aud (audience), and `iss` (issuers claim) from the decoded values and compare them to the configured Amazon Cognito user pool expected values** is incorrect. This solution lacks the key step of signature verification. The integrity of the token cannot be guaranteed unless the signature is verified and the possibility of manipulation exists.
    
    The option that says: **Download and store the corresponding public JSON Web Key (JWK) for the Amazon Cognito user pool. Search the public JSON Web Key for a Key ID that matches the Key ID of the JWT header** is incorrect. The full process of signature verification is not covered by this option, which only focuses on getting and verifying the key ID. The crucial step of using the public key produced from the JWK to validate the token’s signature is missed.
    
- Download and store the corresponding public JSON Web Key (JWK) for the Amazon Cognito user pool. Search the public JSON Web Key for a Key ID that matches the Key ID of the JWT header.
- Get the JSON Web token (JWT) token payload. Decode JWT token using JWT Library. Extract decoded `sub` (User ID), `aud` (audience), and `iss` (issuers claim) from the decoded values and compare them to the configured Amazon Cognito user pool expected values.
- Use `GetUser` API call with the access token to know if the token is expired. Get and compare the `client_id` claim extracted from the access token with the app client ID from the Amazon Cognito user pool.

A company wants to deploy a time-tracking application to an EC2 instance that will be accessed privately by its employees. The instance will be hosted on a Virtual Private Cloud (VPC) with IPv4 and IPv6 networks. Its security engineer must ensure that the instance has IPv6 connectivity to the internet in order to retrieve updates; however, external applications should be unable to establish a connection to the instance.

What should the security engineer do to satisfy the requirements?

- **Attach an egress-only internet gateway to the VPC. Deploy the instance to a private subnet with a custom route table that sends all IPv6 traffic to the gateway.**
    
    An instance in your public subnet can connect to the internet through the internet gateway if it has a public IPv4 address or an IPv6 address. Similarly, resources on the internet can initiate a connection to your instance using its public IPv4 address or its IPv6 address; for example, when you connect to your instance using your local computer.
    
    IPv6 addresses are globally unique and are therefore public by default. If you want your instance to be able to access the internet but you want to prevent resources on the internet from initiating communication with your instance, you can use an **egress-only internet gateway**.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/egress-only-igw_10SEP2023.png)
    
    After creating an egress-only internet gateway, create a custom route table and associate it with your EC2 instance’s subnet, then add a route that points all internet-bound IPv6 traffic (`::/0`) to an egress-only internet gateway in the VPC just like in the diagram above.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Attach an egress-only internet gateway to the VPC. Deploy the instance to a private subnet with a custom route table that sends all IPv6 traffic to the gateway.**
    
    NAT Gateways do not support IPv6, hence, the following options are incorrect:
    
    **– Attach a NAT gateway to the VPC. Deploy the instance to a private subnet with a custom route table that sends all IPv6 traffic to the gateway.**
    
    **– Attach a NAT gateway to the VPC. Deploy the instance to a public subnet with a custom route table that sends all IPv6 traffic to the gateway.**
    
    The option that says: **Deploy the instance to a public subnet and attach a security group that allows all inbound IPv6 traffic** is incorrect as this configuration will allow external applications to initiate a connection with the EC2 instance.
    
- Attach a NAT gateway to the VPC. Deploy the instance to a private subnet with a custom route table that sends all IPv6 traffic to the gateway.
- Attach a NAT gateway to the VPC. Deploy the instance to a public subnet with a custom route table that sends all IPv6 traffic to the gateway.
- Deploy the instance to a public subnet and attach a security group that allows all inbound IPv6 traffic.

A Software Engineer has developed a web application to monitor the pending/processed orders on the corporate sales server. The application needs to be accessed by Delivery, Finance, and Admin teams. The Security Administrator decided to integrate Amazon Cognito to the application to provide user sign-in functionality for the members of each team. The Delivery team should be able to update entries on the application while the Finance team only needs read permissions to verify the flow of orders.

Which of the following options will help the Administrator grant distinct permissions for each team member?

- **Amazon Cognito User Pool Groups**
    
    **Amazon Cognito User Pool** supports “groups” which enables you to create and manage groups, add users to groups, and remove users from groups. Use groups to create collections of users to manage their permissions or to represent different types of users. You can assign an AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role to a group to define the permissions for members of a group. You can use groups to create a collection of users in a user pool, which is often done to set the permissions for those users.
    
    For example, you can create separate groups for users who are readers, contributors, and editors of your website and app. Using the IAM role associated with a group, you can also set different permissions for those different groups so that only contributors can put content into Amazon S3 and only editors can publish content through an API in Amazon API Gateway.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/amazon-cognito-user-pool-create-group-groups-tab_10SEP2023.png)
    
    A user pool is a user directory in Amazon Cognito. With a user pool, your users can sign in to your web or mobile app through Amazon Cognito, or federate through a third-party identity provider (IdP). Whether your users sign-in directly or through a third party, all members of the user pool have a directory profile that you can access through an SDK.
    
    An identity pool, on the other hand, enables your users to obtain temporary AWS credentials to access AWS services, such as Amazon S3 and DynamoDB. Identity pools support anonymous guest users, as well as various identity providers that you can use to authenticate users for identity pools.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Amazon Cognito User Pool Groups.**
    
    **Amazon Cognito Identity Pool** is incorrect because Identity pools are primarily used for authorization (access control) only to specific AWS resources. You can use identity pools to create unique identities for users and give them access to other AWS services. In this use case, you need authentication (identity verification) so you need to use user pool groups.
    
    **Amazon Cognito Sync** is incorrect because this is just a client library that enables cross-device syncing of application-related user data. Cognito Sync API is primarily used to synchronize user data across devices.
    
    **Amazon Cognito Federated Identities** is incorrect because this is the same as Identity Pools. Amazon Cognito identity pools (federated identities) enable you to create unique identities for your users and federate them with identity providers. With an identity pool, you can obtain temporary, limited-privilege AWS credentials to access other AWS services.
    
- Amazon Cognito Identity Pool
- Amazon Cognito Sync
- Amazon Cognito Federated Identities

A company has a number of EC2 instances running in its AWS account. After receiving an alert from Amazon GuardDuty about an `UnauthorizedAccess:EC2/TorClient` finding, a security team wants to determine whether the flagged instance is compromised or not. The company’s AWS account has VPC Flow logs, AWS Config, Amazon Detective, and Amazon Inspector enabled.

What steps should the security team take to gather this information?

- Review the configuration of the flagged EC2 instance using AWS Config and compare it to a known secure baseline to determine whether it is compromised.
- **Investigate the VPC Flow logs using Amazon Detective. Use the `From Findings` annotations to see the log entries involved in the finding.**
    
    Amazon Detective simplifies the investigative process and helps security teams conduct faster and more effective investigations. With the Amazon Detective prebuilt data aggregations, summaries, and context, you can quickly analyze and determine the nature and extent of possible security issues. Detective automatically collects VPC flow logs from your monitored accounts, aggregates them by EC2 instance, and presents visual summaries and analytics about these network flows.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-Detective-Findings.png)
    
    By investigating the VPC flow logs, the security team can determine the source and extent of the unauthorized access, and identify the affected EC2 instance. Furthermore, the <code>From Findings</code> annotations will help the security team quickly filter the logs to the relevant entries.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Investigate the VPC Flow logs using Amazon Detective. Use the `From Findings` annotation to see the log entries involved in the finding.**
    
    The option that says: **Review the configuration of the flagged EC2 instance using AWS Config and compare it to a known secure baseline to determine whether it is compromised** is incorrect. AWS Config may be useful for determining whether the instance is configured securely, but it is unlikely to provide information about unauthorized access through the Tor network.
    
    The option that says: **Generate findings based on the VPC Flow logs using AWS Security Hub, then leverage the security score provided by the service to determine whether the flagged instance is compromised** is incorrect. Although AWS Security Hub can help you identify security issues and vulnerabilities across an AWS environment, it may not provide the detailed information necessary to investigate whether the flagged EC2 instance is compromised.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Inspector to assess the finding generated by Amazon GuardDuty. Determine whether the flagged instance is compromised based on the Amazon Inspector’s risk score** is incorrect. Amazon Inspector is an automated vulnerability management service that continually scans EC2 instances, Lambda functions, and container workloads for software vulnerabilities and unintended network exposure. However, it is not specifically designed to identify unauthorized access through the Tor network or to analyze VPC flow logs.
    
- Generate findings based on the VPC Flow logs using AWS Security Hub, then leverage the security score provided by the service to determine whether the flagged instance is compromised.
- Use Amazon Inspector to assess the finding generated by Amazon GuardDuty. Determine whether the flagged instance is compromised based on the Amazon Inspector’s risk score.

A company is running a host intrusion detection system (HIDS) on its EC2 instances to identify malicious activities. These EC2 instances receive traffic from an Application Load Balancer (ALB). The company’s security engineer must employ additional security features to improve user privacy without interfering with the HIDS.

Which is the MOST secure approach to accomplish the task?

- Add a TLS listener on the ALB that uses Elliptic Curve Diffie-Hellman (ECDHE) cipher suites. Terminate and decrypt the connection at the EC2 instances.
- Add an HTTPS listener on the ALB that uses non-Elliptic Curve Diffie-Hellman (ECDHE) cipher suites. Terminate and decrypt the connection at the EC2 instances.
- **Add an HTTPS listener on the ALB that uses Elliptic Curve Diffie-Hellman (ECDHE) cipher suites, send encrypted traffic to the EC2 instances, and enable Perfect Forward Secrecy (PFS).**
    
    **Perfect Forward Secrecy**, also known as Forward Secrecy, is a feature that uses a derived session key to provide additional safeguards against the eavesdropping of encrypted data. This prevents the decoding of captured data, even if the secret long-term key is compromised.
    
    To begin applying Perfect Forward Secrecy in ALB, create an HTTPS listener and configure your load balancer with a security policy that uses an Elliptic Curve Cryptography (ECDHE) cipher suite.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws-elb-fs-security-policy.JPG)
    
    Note that to use an HTTPS listener, you must deploy at least one SSL/TLS server certificate on your load balancer. The ALB uses a server certificate to terminate the front-end connection and then decrypt requests from clients before sending them to the instances. If you want to re-encrypt the connection from ALB to the instances, deploy an SSL/TLS certificate on your web server and configure it to use HTTPS. Furthermore, you can also configure PFS for additional security.
    
    Unlike a Network Intrusion Detection System (NIDS), a Host Intrusion Detection System (HIDS) can inspect data that might’ve been encrypted in transit. HIDS reads data at the system memory once it’s already been decrypted so encrypting connections between ALB and instances will not interfere with how HIDS operates.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Add an HTTPS listener on the ALB that uses Elliptic Curve Diffie-Hellman (ECDHE) cipher suites, send encrypted traffic to the EC2 instances and enable Perfect Forward Secrecy (PFS).**
    
    The option that says: **Add a TLS listener on the ALB that uses Elliptic Curve Diffie-Hellman (ECDHE) cipher suites. Terminate and decrypt the connection at the EC2 instances** is incorrect. ALB only supports HTTP and HTTPS listeners. Adding a TLS listener is a configuration of a Network Load Balancer.
    
    The option that says: **Add an HTTPS listener on the ALB that uses Elliptic Curve Diffie-Hellman (ECDHE) cipher suites. Decrypt the traffic at the ALB and enable Perfect Forward Secrecy (PFS) on the EC2 instances** is incorrect. Although this is a valid solution, it’s not the most secure among the given options.
    
    The option that says: **Add an HTTPS listener on the ALB that uses non-Elliptic Curve Diffie-Hellman (ECDHE) cipher suites. Terminate and decrypt the connection at the EC2 instances** is incorrect. You have to configure the ALB to use an Elliptic Curve Diffie-Hellman (ECDHE) cipher suite in order to enable Perfect Forward Secrecy. Aside from the protection that encryption offers, this will further augment the privacy of communication between clients and ALB.
    
- Add an HTTPS listener on the ALB that uses Elliptic Curve Diffie-Hellman (ECDHE) cipher suites. Decrypt the traffic at the ALB and enable Perfect Forward Secrecy (PFS) on the EC2 instances.

An organization is in the process of gradually migrating its on-premises file storage to Amazon EFS. The Security Engineer noticed that the created AWS file system was unencrypted. To comply with the Sarbanes-Oxley (SOX) audit requirements, all files stored in the file system must be encrypted with a regularly rotated encryption key.

Which of the following options would satisfy this requirement?

- Create an AWS KMS customer-managed encryption key and enable automatic rotation. Configure the existing EFS file system to turn on data encryption.
- Configure Amazon EFS to invoke an AWS Lambda function to perform the encryption of files using AWS Backup Vault Lock.
- **Create an AWS KMS customer-managed encryption key and configure automatic rotation. Deploy a new EFS file system and enable encryption using the encrypted key.**
    
    **Amazon Elastic File System (EFS)** is a scalable and fully managed cloud-based file storage service provided by Amazon Web Services. It offers shared file storage for multiple EC2 instances, allowing concurrent access to the same file system. With automatic scaling of storage capacity and high availability, Amazon EFS ensures reliable and efficient file storage for applications.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/Amazon-EFS-Encrypted.png)
    
    You cannot change the encryption setting of an EFS file system once it has been created. This means that you cannot encrypt an unencrypted file system by modifying it. You must instead create a new encrypted file system.
    
    When utilizing an encrypted file system, both data and metadata are automatically encrypted before being written to the file system. Likewise, during the reading process, data and metadata are decrypted on-the-fly and presented to the application seamlessly. Amazon EFS handles these encryption and decryption tasks transparently, eliminating the need for any modifications to be made in your applications.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create an AWS KMS customer-managed encryption key and configure automatic rotation. Deploy a new EFS file system and enable encryption using the encrypted key.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an AWS KMS customer-managed encryption key and enable automatic rotation. Configure the existing EFS file system to turn on data encryption** is incorrect because you can’t change the encryption settings of an existing EFS. Instead, create a new EFS with encryption turned on by default.
    
    The option that says: **Configure the existing EFS file system to turn on data encryption. Set up the AWS Config rule `aws-config-aws-kms-policy-rule` to identify non-compliant resources** is incorrect. Just like the option above, you need to create a new EFS file system.
    
    The option that says: **Configure Amazon EFS to invoke an AWS Lambda function to perform the encryption of files using AWS Backup Vault Lock** is incorrect because you can’t directly invoke an AWS Lambda function using Amazon EFS. Lambda functions are typically triggered by events from various sources such as API Gateway, S3, CloudWatch, and others. Take note that AWS Backup Vault Lock is simply an optional feature of a backup vault that provides additional security and control over your backup vaults and not for encrypting your data.
    
- Configure the existing EFS file system to turn on data encryption. Set up the AWS Config rule `aws-config-aws-kms-policy-rule` to identify non-compliant resources.

The IT Security team recently discovered a vulnerability in the old operating systems of several EC2 instances. The team needs to immediately mitigate the security risk to safeguard the company’s applications from various cyber security attacks. For audit purposes, there is also a need to record all of the changes to patch and association compliance statuses.

Which of the following is the MOST suitable solution in this scenario?

- Deploy the OS patches for the outdated EC2 instances using AWS Systems Manager Sessions Manager. Integrate Amazon QuickSight with Kibana to record and track any configuration changes.
- Set up AWS Systems Manager Patch Manager to deploy the patches for the EC2 instances with old operating systems. Use Amazon OpenSearch to record and track any configuration changes.
- **Use AWS Systems Manager Patch Manager to easily deploy the OS patches for the outdated EC2 instances. Set up AWS Config to record and track any configuration changes.**
    
    **AWS Systems Manager Patch Manager** automates the process of patching managed instances with security-related updates. For Linux-based instances, you can also install patches for non-security updates. You can patch fleets of Amazon EC2 instances or your on-premises servers and virtual machines (VMs) by operating system type. This includes supported versions of Windows, Ubuntu Server, Red Hat Enterprise Linux (RHEL), SUSE Linux Enterprise Server (SLES), Amazon Linux, and Amazon Linux 2. You can scan instances to see only a report of missing patches, or you can scan and automatically install all missing patches.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/how-it-works_10SEP2023.png)
    
    Since you are also required to record all of the changes to patch and association compliance statuses, you can use AWS Config to meet this requirement. AWS Config is a service that enables you to assess, audit, and evaluate the configurations of your AWS resources. Config continuously monitors and records your AWS resource configurations and allows you to automate the evaluation of recorded configurations against desired configurations.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Systems Manager Patch Manager to easily deploy the OS patches for the outdated EC2 instances. Set up AWS Config to record and track any configuration changes.**
    
    The option that says: **Set up AWS Systems Manager Patch Manager to deploy the patches for the EC2 instances with old operating systems. Use Amazon OpenSearch to record and track any configuration changes** is incorrect. Although using AWS Systems Manager Patch Manager is right, the service used to record and track any configuration changes is wrong. Amazon OpenSearch is just an AWS-managed service that makes it easy to deploy, operate, and scale OpenSearch clusters in the AWS Cloud.
    
    The option that says: ****Deploy the OS patches for the outdated EC2 instances using AWS Systems Manager Sessions Manager. Integrate Amazon QuickSight with Kibana to record and track any configuration changes** is incorrect. AWS Systems Manager Session Manager simply lets you manage your EC2 instances, on-premises instances, and virtual machines (VMs) through an interactive one-click browser-based shell or through the AWS CLI. The Patch Manager service is a more suitable option for this scenario. In addition, QuickSight and Kibana are primarily used for data visualization and not for patch management. You can use Amazon OpenSearch with Kibana but this service is not suitable for this scenario.
    
    The option that says: **Configure the Amazon EC2 instances to automatically install the OS patches on a weekly basis** is incorrect because Amazon EC2 doesn’t have a built-in function to automatically install the security OS patch on a weekly basis. You have to use AWS Systems Manager Patch Manager instead.
    
- Configure the Amazon EC2 instances to automatically install the OS patches on a weekly basis.

A security engineer has been assigned to build a solution for encrypting data that are stored in AWS. The solution must be interoperable with Amazon S3 and Amazon EBS. For compliance reasons, the use of a hardware security module certified to Federal Information Processing Standards (FIPS) 140-2 Level 3 is required.

Which solution fits these criteria?

- **Create a customer-managed KMS key backed by a custom key store in an AWS CloudHSM cluster.**
    
    By default, your KMS key is protected by hardware security modules (HSMs) that are FIPS 140-2 validated cryptographic modules, however, if you require even more control of the HSMs, you can create a custom key store that is backed by **FIPS 140-2 Level 3 HSM using AWS Cloud HSM**. Each custom key store is backed by an AWS CloudHSM cluster and enables you to generate store, and use your KMS keys in hardware security modules (HSMs) that you control. The KMS custom key store helps satisfy compliance obligations that would otherwise require the use of on-premises HSMs and supports AWS services and encryption toolkits that are integrated with KMS.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/KMS_01_10SEP2023.png)
    
    When you use a KMS key in a custom key store, the cryptographic operations under that key are performed in your AWS CloudHSM cluster. KMS keys stored in a custom key store can be used like any other KMS key, meaning you can use it with any AWS service that has integration with AWS KMS such as Amazon S3 and Amazon EBS.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a customer-managed KMS key backed by a custom key store in an AWS CloudHSM cluster.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a customer-managed KMS key using the KMS-provided key material using the AWS Security Hub** is incorrect because although the default KMS key store supports FIPS 140-2 Level 3, the use of AWS Security Hub is not required
    
    The option that says: **Use the AWS Encryption SDK to encrypt files before sending them to Amazon S3** is incorrect. The AWS Encryption SDK is simply a library for client-side data encryption and decryption. Although it can encrypt files, it does not meet the requirement of securing company-managed keys with a FIPS 140-2 Level 3 validated HSM.
    
    The option that says: **Store the keys in AWS CloudHSM and use the AWS SDK to perform cryptographic operations** is incorrect. The CloudHSM APIs that come with the AWS SDK does not have the capability to perform cryptographic operations. The actions that the AWS SDK supports are simply for HSM cluster management. You have to actually connect to your cluster and use the CloudHSM Client SDK to perform cryptographic operations.
    
- Create a customer-managed KMS key using the KMS-provided key material using the AWS Security Hub
- Store the keys in AWS CloudHSM and use the AWS SDK to perform cryptographic operations.
- Use the AWS Encryption SDK to encrypt files before sending them to Amazon S3.

An organization currently has 100 IAM users and plans to implement the principle of least privilege access in its AWS account. The InfoSec team has been tasked with conducting annual audits of IAM permissions. Each IAM identity must be reviewed, and any unused permissions must be deleted.

Which of the following options should be implemented for this scenario?

- Use AWS Config to audit IAM users and delete unused permissions.
- Use AWS Trusted Advisor security check to review the permissions of IAM users.
- Use AWS CloudTrail to track account activity and delete unused permissions of IAM users.
- **Use AWS IAM Access Analyzer to review last-accessed information and delete unused permissions.**
    
    **AWS IAM Access Analyzer** is a service that helps you review the access granted to your resources and identify unintended access or unused permissions. It enables you to review the last accessed information for your resources and remove any unnecessary or unused permissions. It simplifies the process of maintaining the principle of least privilege by highlighting potential security vulnerabilities and guiding you in taking appropriate remediation actions. By leveraging Access Analyzer, you can proactively strengthen the security of your AWS environment, reduce the risk of unauthorized access, and ensure compliance with security best practices.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-IAM-Access-Analyzer.png)
    
    In certain scenarios, it is possible to assign permissions to entities (users or roles) that exceed their actual requirements. IAM offers last-accessed information to assist in identifying and removing unused permissions. By utilizing this information, you can fine-tune your policies and grant access only to the services and actions that are actually utilized by your entities. This approach promotes adherence to the best practice of least privilege.
    
    By using IAM Access Analyzer, you can review the last accessed information for IAM identities and identify any unused permissions. This allows you to remove unnecessary permissions, reducing the attack surface and minimizing the risk of unauthorized access or accidental misuse of resources.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS IAM Access Analyzer to review last-accessed information and delete unused permissions.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Config to audit IAM users and delete unused permissions** is incorrect because AWS Config focuses more on configuration drift and policy compliance rather than tracking individual IAM identity permissions.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS CloudTrail to track account activity and delete unused permissions of IAM users** is incorrect. Although you can use CloudTrail to record IAM user actions, it still can’t provide information about unused permissions of IAM users.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS Trusted Advisor security check to review the permissions of IAM users** is incorrect because there is no security check that provides a comprehensive view of the permissions used and unused by each IAM identity.
    

A security engineer is designing a monitoring solution for expiring public certificates managed by AWS Certificate Manager (ACM). The certificates are a mixture of imported and managed certificates that were validated thru either DNS or email methods. The security engineer must receive alerts by email when a certificate is nearing its expiration date.

What is the MOST operationally efficient way to meet this requirement?

- In AWS Security Hub, enable the AWS Foundational Security Best Practices standard with integrated ACM. Create a custom action rule that matches the `NotBefore` attribute pattern in the ACM findings, and use an Amazon SNS topic as the target.
- Build an Amazon CloudWatch alarm based on the `DaysToExpiry` metric for each certificate ARNs in the AWS/CertificateManager namespace. Configure the alarm to publish a notification to an Amazon SNS topic when the value for the `DaysToExpiry` metric is less than 30 days.
- Write a Lambda function that lists all certificates and filters on the `NotAfter` attribute to determine the expiration date. Use the AWS SDK for SNS to send email notifications. Use an Amazon EventBridge Events cron expression to run the function daily.
- **Create an Amazon EventBridge rule with the `ACM Certificate Approaching Expiration`as the event pattern and configure an Amazon SNS topic as the target.**
    
    ACM sends daily expiration events for all active certificates (public, private and imported) starting 45 days prior to expiration. This timing can be changed using the `PutAccountConfiguration` ACM API. ACM automatically initiates the renewal of eligible certificates (for DNS-validated certificates) that it issued, but imported certificates need to be re-issued and re-imported prior to expiration to avoid outages.
    
    When an ACM certificate is approaching its expiration date, ACM emits an event to EventBridge with the `ACM Certificate Approaching Expiration` event pattern.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/eventbridge-aacm-certificate-approaching-expiration-event-pattern.jpg)
    
    You can create an EventBridge rule to match this event pattern, which will then forward it to an Amazon SNS topic that you specify. You can configure the SNS topic to send an email notification to the security engineer when the event is received.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create an Amazon EventBridge rule with the `ACM Certificate Approaching Expiration` as the event pattern and configure an Amazon SNS topic as the target.**
    
    The option that says: **Write a Lambda function that lists all certificates and filters on the `NotAfter`attribute to determine the expiration date. Use the AWS SDK for SNS to send email notifications. Use an Amazon EventBridge Events cron expression to run the function daily** is incorrect. Although this solution is valid, it’s not the most operationally efficient since it requires writing custom code for the Lambda function to filter expiring certificates.
    
    The option that says: **Build an Amazon CloudWatch alarm based on the `DaysToExpiry` metric for each certificate ARNs in the AWS/CertificateManager namespace. Configure the alarm to publish a notification to an Amazon SNS topic when the value for the `DaysToExpiry` metric is less than 30 days** is incorrect. This is also valid. However, it’s not operationally efficient if you were to monitor multiple certificates because you can only create an alarm for a single metric. This means that you would have to build an alarm for each certificate you’re monitoring for.
    
    The option that says: **In AWS Security Hub, enable the AWS Foundational Security Best Practices standard with integrated ACM. Create a custom action rule that matches the `NotBefore` attribute pattern in the ACM findings, and use an Amazon SNS topic as the target** is incorrect because this would just generate a finding when a certificate is about to expire. Furthermore, findings must be sent to Amazon EventBridge, not Amazon SNS.
    

A Security Engineer found out that API logging was disabled in the corporate AWS production account. The Engineer also noticed that the root IAM user was used to create new API keys without approval.

What should the Engineer do to detect and automatically remediate these types of security incidents?

- Set up a new CloudTrail event that detects the deactivation of CloudTrail logs. Create another CloudTrail event that detects the creation of root API keys. Set up an AWS Lambda function to re-enable CloudTrail logs and deactivate the root API keys.
- Review all of the API calls using Amazon Inspector. Configure the inspector agent to leverage in Amazon SNS topics to notify the Security Engineer of the change to CloudTrail. Revoke the new API keys created by the root user using the inspector agent.
- **Create a config rule in AWS Config to detect when CloudTrail is disabled. Use another config rule to detect the presence of root user access keys. Set up an AWS Lambda function to re-enable CloudTrail logs and deactivate the root API keys.**
    
    **AWS Config** provides *AWS managed rules*, which are predefined, customizable rules that AWS Config uses to evaluate whether your AWS resources comply with common best practices. For example, you could use a managed rule to quickly start assessing whether your Amazon Elastic Block Store (Amazon EBS) volumes are encrypted or whether specific tags are applied to your resources.
