You can set up and activate these rules without writing the code to create an AWS Lambda function, which is required if you want to create custom rules. The AWS Config console guides you through the process of configuring and activating a managed rule. You can also use the AWS Command Line Interface or AWS Config API to pass the JSON code that defines your configuration of a managed rule.
    
    ![AWS Config Rules](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/td-aws-config-rules-1July2025.jpg)
    
    You can customize the behavior of a managed rule to suit your needs. For example, you can define the rule’s scope to constrain which resources trigger an evaluation for the rule, such as EC2 instances or volumes. You can customize the rule’s parameters to define attributes that your resources must have to comply with the rule. For example, you can customize a parameter to specify that your security group should block incoming traffic to a specific port number.
    
    In this scenario, you can use these two AWS Config Managed Rules:
    
    **iam-root-access-key-check** – Checks whether the root user access key is available
    
    **cloudtrail-enabled** – Checks whether CloudTrail is enabled in your AWS account
    
    Alternatively, for real-time detection of root API key creation, you can use AWS CloudTrail with Amazon EventBridge to monitor the `CreateAccessKey` API call made by the root user. An EventBridge rule can trigger an AWS Lambda function to deactivate the keys immediately.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a config rule in AWS Config to detect when CloudTrail is disabled. Use another config rule to detect the presence of root user access keys. Set up an AWS Lambda function to re-enable CloudTrail logs and deactivate the root API keys.** The AWS Lambda function can use AWS SDK calls, such as `StartLogging` to re-enable CloudTrail and `DeleteAccessKey` to deactivate root API keys, ensuring automated remediation.
    
    The option that says: **Review all of the API calls using Amazon Inspector. Configure the inspector agent to leverage in Amazon SNS topics to notify the Security Engineer of the change to CloudTrail. Revoke the new API keys created by the root user using the inspector agent** is incorrect because Amazon inspector is only suitable when monitoring and running security assessments to your EC2 instances.
    
    The option that says: **Create an Amazon CloudWatch event that detects CloudTrail deactivation. Use Amazon Trusted Advisor Checks to automatically detect the creation of root API keys. Set up an AWS Lambda function to re-enable CloudTrail logs and deactivate the root API keys** is incorrect because Amazon Trusted Advisor is simply not capable of automatically detecting the creation of root API Keys.
    
    The option that says: **Set up a new CloudTrail event that detects the deactivation of CloudTrail logs. Create another CloudTrail event that detects the creation of root API keys. Set up an AWS Lambda function to re-enable CloudTrail logs and deactivate the root API keys** is incorrect because you can’t use CloudTrail to monitor itself. You have to use AWS Config to check whether AWS CloudTrail is enabled on your AWS accounts.
    
- Create an Amazon CloudWatch event that detects CloudTrail deactivation. Use Amazon Trusted Advisor Checks to automatically detect the creation of root API keys. Set up an AWS Lambda function to re-enable CloudTrail logs and deactivate the root API keys.

A company has an AWS organization with over 100+ accounts managed by the AWS Organizations service. The company also configured a member account as an Amazon GuardDuty delegated administrator account. The SecOps team established trusted access between GuardDuty and the Organizations from the management account.

The GuardDuty service already generates report findings for the whole organization. These GuardDuty findings are also sent to the AWS Security Hub, aggregating other reports from Amazon Inspector and Amazon Macie. To further improve the company’s security posture, the SecOps team wants to receive notification alerts via email for all the findings ranging from medium to high severity levels from GuardDuty.

Which of the following provides the MOST operationally efficient solution that can satisfy the given requirements?

- In the management account, enable trusted access for AWS CloudTrail. Create a rule in Amazon EventBridge that reacts to the `GetFindings` API call. Create an Amazon SNS topic and subscribe to the SecOps team’s email address for the topic. Configure the SNS Topic as the target of the EventBridge rule.
- In the management account, enable trusted access for AWS Config. Create a rule for `guardduty-non-archived-findings`. Set up a rule in Amazon EventBridge that reacts to Config NON_COMPLIANT for medium and high severity levels events. Create an Amazon SNS topic and subscribe the SecOps team email address to the topic. Set the SNS Topic as the target of the Config rule.
- **In the GuardDuty delegated administrator account, create a rule in Amazon EventBridge that reacts to medium to high severity level findings. Create an Amazon SNS topic and subscribe to the SecOps team’s email address for the topic. Configure the EventBridge rule to set the SNS Topic as the target.**
    
    Amazon GuardDuty is a continuous security monitoring service that analyzes and processes a variety data sources, using threat intelligence feeds and machine learning to identify unexpected and potentially unauthorized and malicious activity within your AWS environment.
    
    You can help simplify the management of GuardDuty by using Organizations to manage GuardDuty across all of the accounts in your organization. When you use GuardDuty with an AWS Organizations organization, you can designate any account within the organization to be the GuardDuty delegated administrator.
    
    ![Amazon GuardDuty & EventBridge](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws_guardduty_eventbridge_jul2023.png)
    
    An account that gets designated as a delegated administrator becomes a GuardDuty administrator account, has GuardDuty enabled automatically in the designated AWS Region, and also has the permission to enable and manage GuardDuty for all of the accounts in the organization within that Region. The other accounts in the organization can be viewed and added as GuardDuty member accounts associated with this delegated administrator account.
    
    You can use Amazon EventBridge with GuardDuty to set up automated finding alerts by sending GuardDuty finding events to a messaging hub to help increase the visibility of GuardDuty findings. You can send findings alerts to email or Slack by setting up an SNS topic and then connecting that topic to an Amazon EventBridge event rule.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **In the GuardDuty delegated administrator account, create a rule in Amazon EventBridge that reacts to medium to high severity level findings. Create an Amazon SNS topic and subscribe to the SecOps team’s email address for the topic. Configure the EventBridge rule to set the SNS Topic as the target.**
    
    The option that says: **In the GuardDuty delegated administrator account, create a rule in Amazon EventBridge that runs on a daily basis. Create an Amazon SNS topic and subscribe to the SecOps team’s email addresses. Create an AWS Lambda function to call the `ListFindings` API call and send a notification using the SNS Topic. Configure to set the Lambda as the target of the rule** is incorrect. Calling the `ListFindings` API with severity `findingCriteria` will definitely give the finding IDs of all medium and high-severity findings; however, this implementation lacks consistency, as you would have to create a custom code for the function. This solution has more operational overhead and adds more complexity.
    
    The option that says: **In the management account, enable trusted access for AWS Config. Create a rule for `guardduty-non-archived-findings`. Set up a rule in Amazon EventBridge that reacts to Config NON_COMPLIANT for medium and high severity levels events. Create an Amazon SNS topic and subscribe the SecOps team email address to the topic. Set the SNS Topic as the target of the Config rule** is incorrect. Even though this seems to be a valid solution, using the `guardduty-non-archived-findings` managed rule will just allow the SecOps team to see the particular findings that are not archived. Usually, these findings have not been closed or investigated yet. This is unnecessary because Amazon EventBridge can be directly configured with the GuardDuty Findings event. This implementation has unnecessarily more management overhead.
    
    The option that says: **In the management account, enable trusted access for AWS CloudTrail. Create a rule in Amazon EventBridge that reacts to the `GetFindings` API call. Create an Amazon SNS topic and subscribe to the SecOps team’s email address for the topic. Configure the SNS Topic as the target of the EventBridge rule** is incorrect. While creating an Amazon EventBridge rule is a step in the right direction, configuring it to react to `GetFindings` An API call will not help the SecOps Team know the list of findings at the medium to high-severity level. A more suitable solution is to simply use the `ListFindings` API call instead. Either way, this implementation is still inferior to the correct answer as this introduces more operational overhead.
    
- In the GuardDuty delegated administrator account, create a rule in Amazon EventBridge that runs on a daily basis. Create an Amazon SNS topic and subscribe to the SecOps team’s email addresses. Create an AWS Lambda function to call the `ListFindings` API call and send a notification using the SNS Topic. Configure to set the Lambda as the target of the rule.

A company manages multiple AWS accounts in AWS Organizations to handle critical data and transactional systems. They deal with a significant volume of sensitive information. The company stores data in both Amazon S3 and Amazon DynamoDB. Data processing and analysis are using AWS Lambda.

To ensure the utmost data security, the company needs to implement a solution that encrypts all sensitive data at rest and enforces the principle of least privilege data access controls. The company has created a customer-managed key in AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) for encryption purposes and must use the key for all encryptions.

What should the company do next to meet these requirements?

- Create an S3 Bucket policy to require the use of the KMS key using `s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption`. Enforce an SCP that denies S3 bucket and DynamoDB table creation lacking encryption with the key.
- Enable server-side encryption for Amazon S3 buckets and Amazon DynamoDB. Attach an IAM policy that allows `kms:Decrypt` action to a Lambda IAM role. Set up an AWS Config rule to issue alerts for resources lacking encryption with the key.
- **Create a key policy for the customer-managed key permitting the `kms:Decrypt` action for Amazon S3, DynamoDB, and Lambda. Enforce an SCP that denies S3 bucket and DynamoDB table creation lacking encryption with the key.**
    
    A **key policy** is a resource policy for an AWS KMS key. Key policies are the primary way to control access to KMS keys. Every KMS key must have exactly one key policy. The statements in the key policy determine who has permission to use the KMS key and how they can use it. You can also use IAM policies and grants to control access to the KMS key, but every KMS key must have a key policy.
    
    Unless the key policy explicitly allows it, you cannot use IAM policies to allow access to a KMS key. Without permission from the key policy, IAM policies that allow permissions have no effect.
    
    Many AWS services use AWS KMS keys to protect the resources they manage. When you use a customer managed key with an AWS service, you set and maintain the key policy. That key policy must allow the service the minimum permissions that it requires to protect the resource on your behalf. AWS recommends that you follow the principle of least privilege: give the service only the permissions that it requires.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/KMS_Key_Policy_BJL_26072023.png)
    
    In order to encrypt all sensitive data at rest and enforce the principle of least privilege data access controls, you should allow the AWS Services to use the customer-managed KMS key and enforce an SCP that denies S3 Bucket and DynamoDB table creation lacking encryption with the key.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create a key policy for the customer-managed key permitting the `kms:Decrypt` action for Amazon S3, DynamoDB, and Lambda. Enforce an SCP that denies S3 bucket and DynamoDB table creation lacking encryption with the key.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an S3 Bucket policy to require the use of the KMS key using `s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption`. Enforce an SCP that denies S3 bucket and DynamoDB table creation lacking encryption with the key** is incorrect. Even though this will make sure that the data encryption is at rest in the S3 buckets, the bucket policy did not particularly specify using the existing customer-managed KMS key. Use `s3:x-amz-server-side-encryption-aws-kms-key-id` instead to require the usage of the key.
    
    The option that says: **Enable server-side encryption for Amazon S3 buckets and Amazon DynamoDB. Set up an AWS Config rule to issue alerts for resources lacking encryption with the key** is incorrect. Enabling server-side encryption for both Amazon S3 and Amazon DynamoDB is a good step to ensure encryption at rest for future data. However, it does not address the requirement to use the customer-managed KMS key for encryption.
    
    The option that says: **Enable server-side encryption for Amazon S3 buckets and Amazon DynamoDB. Attach an IAM policy that allows `km:Decrypt` action to a Lambda IAM role. Set up an AWS Config rule to issue alerts for resources lacking encryption with the key** is incorrect. Same with the previous option, while enabling server-side encryption is a good practice, it doesn’t address the requirement to use the customer-managed KMS key specifically for encryption. Additionally, setting up an AWS Config rule to issue alerts for resources lacking encryption doesn’t explicitly enforce encryption using the customer-managed KMS key for existing or new resources.
    
- Enable server-side encryption for Amazon S3 buckets and Amazon DynamoDB. Set up an AWS Config rule to issue alerts for resources lacking encryption with the key.

A bank operates a critical application on Amazon EC2 instances. The application handles sensitive financial data. The company must ensure strict security measures to protect the data from unauthorized access.

The security team must enforce secure access to the EC2 instances for system administrators without compromising security. They want to avoid opening inbound ports for SSH access, maintain bastion hosts, or manage SSH keys for the EC2 instances. Additionally, the team wants to monitor and record all encrypted session activity logs of system administrators.

Which solution can meet these requirements with the LEAST effort and minimize the security risks?

- **Implement AWS Systems Manager Session Manager to establish connections to the EC2 instances. Set up Amazon CloudWatch logging, enabling the upload session logs option, and ensure that only allow encrypted CloudWatch Logs log groups.**
    
    **Session Manager** is a fully managed AWS Systems Manager capability. With Session Manager, you can manage your Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) instances, edge devices, on-premises servers, and virtual machines (VMs). Session Manager provides secure and auditable node management without the need to open inbound ports, maintain bastion hosts, or manage SSH keys.
    
    To meet operational or security requirements in your organization, you might need to provide a record of the connections made to your managed nodes and the commands that were run on them. Logging and auditing capabilities are provided through integration with AWS services. One of those AWS Services is the **Amazon CloudWatch Logs**. CloudWatch Logs allows you to monitor, store, and access log files from various AWS services. You can send session log data to a CloudWatch Logs log group for debugging and troubleshooting purposes. Log data can be sent to your log group with or without AWS KMS encryption using your KMS key.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Sessions_Manager_CW_Logging_BJL_28072023.png)
    
    You should use AWS Sytems Manager Session Manager to manage the EC2 instances without opening inbound ports for SSH access, maintaining bastion hosts, or managing SSH keys for the EC2 instances. In order to monitor and record all encrypted session activity logs, you should enable CloudWatch logging and allow only encrypted CloudWatch log groups to ensure encryption.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Implement AWS Systems Manager Session Manager to establish connections to the EC2 instances. Set up Amazon CloudWatch logging, enabling the upload session logs option, and ensure that only allow encrypted CloudWatch Logs log groups.**
    
    The option that says: **Implement AWS Systems Manager Session Manager to establish connections to the EC2 instances. Set up Amazon S3 logging, enabling the upload session logs option, and ensure that only allow encrypted S3 buckets** is incorrect. This solution will allow the security administrators to manage instances without opening inbound ports for SSH access, maintaining bastion hosts, or managing SSH keys for the EC2 instances and can also store the activity sessions. However, using an S3 bucket will not satisfy the monitoring of logs requirement because Amazon S3 is mainly for archiving purposes. You must store the session logs in the CloudWatch Logs log group instead to monitor and record all encrypted session activity logs.
    
    The option that says: **Implement AWS Systems Manager Session Manager to establish connections to the EC2 instances. Set up an AWS CloudTrail and configure it to save logs to an encrypted CloudWatch log group** is incorrect. Although this will allow security administrators to manage EC2 instances without opening inbound ports, using Amazon CloudTrail will not allow you to capture the session logs because CloudTrail only logs API activities. Hence, this will not satisfy the requirements for monitoring and recording session logs.
    
    The option that says: **Install CloudWatch Agent to all instances using AWS Systems Manager. Create a Cloudwtach log group, and associate a KMS key for encryption. Send the logs from the instance to the encrypted log group** is incorrect. While it is possible to send logs to an encrypted log group, this solution does not leverage AWS Systems Manager Session Manager, which is required to establish connections to the EC2 instances without opening inbound ports for SSH access.
    
- Implement AWS Systems Manager Session Manager to establish connections to the EC2 instances. Set up an AWS CloudTrail and configure it to save logs to an encrypted CloudWatch log group.
- Implement AWS Systems Manager Session Manager to establish connections to the EC2 instances. Set up Amazon S3 logging, enabling the upload session logs option, and ensure that only allow encrypted S3 buckets.
- Install CloudWatch Agent to all instances using AWS Systems Manager. Create a CloudWatch log group, and associate a KMS key for encryption. Send the logs from the instance to the encrypted log group.

A healthcare organization has a data retention policy where a secure destruction process must be run when the patient data approaches the end of its retention period. The organization needs a cryptographic implementation that can perform cryptographic erasures within at least 7 days when the data is no longer required. An AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) custom key store must be used to manage the KMS keys it created.

Which of the following must be implemented to meet these specifications?

- **Utilize the Customer managed keys in AWS KMS.**
    
    AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) is a managed service that makes it easy for you to create and control the cryptographic keys that are used to protect your data. The KMS keys that you create are customer managed keys. Customer managed keys are KMS keys in your AWS account that you create, own, and manage. Customer managed keys appear on the **Customer managed keys** page of the AWS Management Console for AWS KMS. You have full control over these KMS keys, including establishing and maintaining their key policies, IAM policies, and grants, enabling and disabling them, rotating their cryptographic material, adding tags, creating aliases that refer to the KMS keys, and scheduling the KMS keys for deletion.
    
    AWS KMS never deletes your KMS keys unless you explicitly schedule them for deletion and the mandatory waiting period expires. Because it is destructive and potentially dangerous to delete a KMS key, AWS KMS requires you to set a waiting period of 7 – 30 days.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/kms_key_schedule_deleteion_BJL_23062023.png)
    
    AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) Customer managed keys do not carry out cryptographic erasure directly. However, deleting a KMS key is required for the cryptographic erasure procedure in AWS KMS. The encrypted data protected by the KMS key is unreadable and unrecoverable when the waiting period has passed since the key material is already irreversibly destroyed. This process achieves cryptographic erasure of the data.
    
    Imported key material is supported on all types of KMS keys except for KMS keys in custom key stores. This type of special-purpose key provides you with greater control of your key material. With this, you can perform cryptographic erasures in a matter of minutes and not wait for at least 7 days if you use a customer managed key. The only downside of this is its incompatibility with the custom key store in AWS KMS.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Utilize the Customer managed keys in AWS KMS.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a Hash-Based Message Authentication Code (HMAC) KMS key** is incorrect because Hash-Based Message Authentication Code (HMAC) KMS keys are basically symmetric keys that you can use to generate and verify HMACs within AWS KMS. The scenario didn’t mention any requirement for neither generating nor verifying hash-based message authentication codes; hence, using HMAC is unnecessary.
    
    The option that says: **Make use of AWS managed keys** is incorrect. AWS managed keys are practical for most use cases, but they don’t let you schedule deletion or regulate the cryptographic erasure procedure. AWS automatically handles the deletion and cryptographic erasure of KMS keys it manages, with no option to set a waiting period or initiate the erasure within a defined timeframe.
    
    The option that says: **Use a KMS key with an imported key material** is incorrect. Although you can bring your key material into AWS KMS for encryption using imported key material with KMS key, this type is not compatible with a custom key store in AWS KMS. Take note that the scenario explicitly mentioned that a custom key store must be used to manage the customer master keys.
    
- Create a Hash-Based Message Authentication Code (HMAC) KMS key
- Make use of AWS managed keys.
- Use a KMS key with an imported key material.

A multinational company has a hybrid cloud infrastructure and is actively migrating the majority of its resources to AWS. The Cloud Architect needs to design a solution for detecting and consolidating security findings while ensuring that no public IP addresses are assigned before the Amazon VPC subnets are provisioned.

Which solution will meet this requirement with the LEAST amount of effort?

- Create a Lambda function that will be triggered when an Amazon VPC subnet is launched. Configure AWS Step Functions to send AWS Config rule evaluations to AWS Security Hub.
- Set up AWS Config managed rules with detective evaluation. Use AWS Security Hub to view and manage custom rule evaluation results.
- Create an AWS Config custom rule with detective evaluation. Configure the rule to evaluate Amazon VPC periodically. Use a Lambda function to send the rule evaluations to AWS Security Hub.
- **Set up AWS Config managed rules with proactive evaluation. Use AWS Security Hub to view and manage custom rule evaluation results.**
    
    **AWS Config** is a service that provides detailed inventory, configuration history, and change notifications for resources within an AWS account. It allows users to assess, audit, and evaluate the compliance of their AWS resource configurations against desired configurations, helping to maintain security and operational best practices.
    
    **AWS Security Hub** is a comprehensive security service that provides a centralized view of security alerts and compliance status across an AWS environment. It aggregates findings from various AWS services, partner solutions, and custom integration, allowing users to prioritize and remediate security issues efficiently, and continuously monitor their security posture.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws-security-hub-CIS-AWS-benchmark.JPG)
    
    There are two evaluation modes for AWS Config:
    
    1. **Proactive evaluation** – is used to assess resources before they are deployed. This enables you to determine whether a set of resource properties, if used to define an AWS resource, would be COMPLIANT or NON_COMPLIANT given the proactive rules in your account in your Region.
    
    2. **Detective evaluation** – is used to assess resources that have already been deployed. This allows you to assess the configuration settings of your existing resources.
    
    When a Config rule evaluation passes or fails, a ‘passed’ or ‘failed’ finding for that evaluation will appear in Security Hub. Any changes in the status of the Config rule evaluation will be reflected in the Security Hub finding.
    
    The integration of Security Hub and AWS Config broadens the centralization and single pane of glass experience by combining your Config evaluation results with your other security findings, allowing you to more easily search, triage, investigate, and act on your security findings.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Set up AWS Config managed rules with proactive evaluation. Use AWS Security Hub to view and manage custom rule evaluation results.**
    
    The option that says: **Set up AWS Config managed rules with detective evaluation. Use AWS Security Hub to view and manage custom rule evaluation results** is incorrect because, based on the given requirements, the resources should be evaluated before it is deployed. Take note that detective evaluation assesses resources that have already been provisioned. Therefore, you should use AWS Config with proactive evaluation to assess AWS resources.
    
    The option that says: **Create an AWS Config custom rule with detective evaluation. Configure the rule to evaluate Amazon VPC periodically. Use a Lambda function to send the rule evaluations to AWS Security Hub** is incorrect. Just like the option above, you need to configure proactive evaluation instead of detective evaluation. Also, AWS Security Hub receives AWS Config rule evaluation results as security findings automatically, eliminating the need to create a Lambda function.
    
    The option that says: **Create a Lambda function that will be triggered when an Amazon VPC subnet is launched. Configure AWS Step Functions to send AWS Config rule evaluations to AWS Security Hub** is incorrect because you will be writing custom code instead of using a direct integration for AWS Config and AWS Security Hub. Implementing this option requires a lot of unnecessary configurations.
    

A company has two Amazon EC2 instances hosted in the same VPC but placed in separate Availability Zones. Each instance has a public IP address and is able to connect to the public Internet. The two EC2 instances can communicate with each other via their private IP addresses. However, the Security Engineer noticed that these two instances can’t communicate with each other when using their public IP addresses.

What should the Engineer do to rectify this problem without compromising security?

- Modify both the security groups of the EC2 instances to allow inbound access from `0.0.0.0/0` CIDR IP range.
- **Add an inbound rule in the security group of the first EC2 instance to allow the public IP address of the second EC2 instance and vice versa.**
    
    A security group acts as a virtual firewall for your instance to control inbound and outbound traffic. When you launch an instance in a VPC, you can assign up to five security groups to the instance. Security groups act at the instance level, not the subnet level. Therefore, each instance in a subnet in your VPC could be assigned to a different set of security groups. If you don’t specify a particular group at launch time, the instance is automatically assigned to the default security group for the VPC.
    
    For each security group, you add *rules* that control the inbound traffic to instances, and a separate set of rules that control the outbound traffic. This section describes the basic things you need to know about security groups for your VPC and their rules.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/security-diagram_13SEP2023.png)
    
    Private IPv4 addresses (also referred to as private IP addresses in this topic) are not reachable over the Internet and can be used for communication between the instances in your VPC. When you launch an instance into a VPC, a primary private IP address from the IPv4 address range of the subnet is assigned to the default network interface (eth0) of the instance. Each instance is also given a private (internal) DNS hostname that resolves to the private IP address of the instance. If you don’t specify a primary private IP address, we select an available IP address in the subnet range for you.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is **Add an inbound rule in the security group of the first EC2 instance to allow the public IP address of the second EC2 instance and vice versa.**
    
    The option that says: **Modify both the security groups of the EC2 instances to allow inbound access from `0.0.0.0/0` CIDR IP range** is incorrect because this configuration will allow access from all networks not just from the EC2 instances. Using this specific CIDR IP range will compromise the security of your network since it also allows access from the public Internet.
    
    The option that says: **Attach a NAT Gateway in VPC that will allow two EC2 instances to communicate with each other using their private IP addresses** is incorrect because a NAT Gateway is primarily used to enable instances in a private subnet to connect to the Internet or other AWS services but prevent the Internet from initiating a connection with those instances. The scenario already mentioned that the instances can access the public Internet without any problems.
    
    The option that says: **Create an inbound rule in the security group of the first EC2 instance to allow access from the second EC2 instance based on the Instance ID and vice versa** is incorrect because you can’t use the Instance ID in a security group. The source in the inbound rule of a security group can be a single IP address, a range of addresses (for example, 203.0.113.0/24), or another VPC security group.
    
- Attach a NAT Gateway in VPC that will allow two EC2 instances to communicate with each other using their private IP addresses.
- Create an inbound rule in the security group of the first EC2 instance to allow access from the second EC2 instance based on the Instance ID and vice versa.

A company manages multiple AWS accounts using AWS Organizations. They aim to implement a security monitoring system that can adapt to varying workloads and continuous security threat detection within ap-southeast-1 region. They want to automatically enable the monitoring system to all the member accounts of the organization, including future member accounts.

Which solution that aligns with the AWS security best practices can meet these requirements with the LEAST effort?

- **Designate a member account as delegated administrator from the management account's Amazon GuardDuty console in ap-southeast-1. Log in using the delegated administrator credentials. Turn on auto-enable at Amazon GuardDuty console settings in the ap-southeast-1.**
    
    Amazon GuardDuty is a continuous security monitoring service that analyzes and processes a variety data sources, using threat intelligence feeds and machine learning to identify unexpected and potentially unauthorized and malicious activity within your AWS environment.
    
    You can help simplify management of GuardDuty by using Organizations to manage GuardDuty across all of the accounts in your organization.
    
    When you use GuardDuty with an AWS Organizations organization, you can designate any account within the organization to be the GuardDuty delegated administrator. A delegated administrator is granted permission to enable and manage GuardDuty for all accounts in the organization within the Region.
    
    Your organization’s management account can be the delegated administrator, but this is not recommended based on AWS Security best practices following the principle of least privilege.
    
    To automatically enable Amazon GuardDuty to all existing and new members of the organization, you can navigate to Accounts and turn on Auto Enable to All Accounts.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/GuardDuty_Auto_enable_BJL_27072023.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Designate a member account as delegated administrator from the management account’s Amazon GuardDuty console in ap-southeast-1. Log in using the delegated administrator credentials. Turn on auto-enable at Amazon GuardDuty console settings in the ap-southeast-1.**
    
    The option that says: **Enable AWS Control Tower with ap-southeast-1 as the home region. Use AWS Control Tower Account Factory to create a new account. Configure Account Factory Customization (AFC) section to use a blueprint that enables Amazon GuardDuty. Select deploy to home region, then create an account**is incorrect. Although this is a possible solution, configuring Account Factory Customization (AFC) can indeed enable Amazon GuardDuty to new member accounts, the requirement is limited only to enabling a security monitoring system like Amazon GuardDuty without the need for additional governance features provided by AWS Control Tower. Using Control Tower can be excessive and can introduce unnecessary complexity.
    
    The option that says: **Designate a member account as delegated administrator from the management account’s AWS Security Hub console in ap-southeast-1. Log in using the delegated administrator credentials. Turn on auto-enable at AWS Security Hub console settings in the ap-southeast-1** is incorrect because AWS Security Hub itself does not perform the continuous security threat detection at scale. Instead, it acts as a central hub for aggregating security findings from various integrated AWS services and third-party partner solutions.
    
    The option that says: **Enable AWS CloudFormation Stacksets with trusted access to AWS Organizations. Designate a member account as delegated administrator from the management account. Log in using the delegated administrator credentials. Create a StackSet for enabling Amazon GuardDuty. Enable automatic deployment for future member account deployments** is incorrect. While using AWS CloudFormation StackSets with trusted access to AWS Organizations can help automate deployments, it involves additional steps and configuration to set up StackSets for enabling GuardDuty in member accounts, making it more complex compared to the Amazon GuardDuty option.
    
- Enable AWS Control Tower with ap-southeast-1 as the home region. Use AWS Control Tower Account Factory to create a new account. Configure Account Factory Customization (AFC) section to use a blueprint that enables Amazon GuardDuty. Select deploy to home region, then create an account.
- Designate a member account as delegated administrator from the management account's AWS Security Hub console in ap-southeast-1. Log in using the delegated administrator credentials. Turn on auto-enable at AWS Security Hub console settings in the ap-southeast-1.
- Enable AWS CloudFormation Stacksets with trusted access to AWS Organizations. Designate a member account as delegated administrator from the management account. Log in using the delegated administrator credentials. Create a StackSet for enabling Amazon GuardDuty. Enable automatic deployment for future member account deployments.

A company’s developer has recently finalized an update to a Lambda function in his local machine. A security engineer must ensure any updates to the Lambda function are verified for authenticity before deployment to prevent tampering. The engineer has enabled Code signing for the Lambda function.

Which combination of steps should be taken next to meet the requirement? (Select two)

- Bundle the source code in a folder named `lambda_handler` and upload it to an S3 bucket.
- **Package the source code in a zip file and upload it to an S3 bucket.**
- Digitally sign the code using an AWS KMS asymmetric key. Restrict access to the KMS key to the security engineer.
- **Sign the code using an AWS Signer profile.**
- Use Amazon CodeGuru to detect security vulnerabilities in the code.
- **Evaluation**
    
    Code signing for AWS Lambda helps to ensure that only trusted code runs in your Lambda functions. When you enable code signing for a function, Lambda checks every code deployment and verifies that the code package is signed by a trusted source. Note that functions defined as container images do not support code signing.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-Signer-Profile.PNG)
    
    In the scenario, you must first create an AWS Signer profile and ensure the code uploaded in the S3 bucket is packaged in the ZIP format. You can then set the Signer profile in the code signing option of the Lambda function. This way, when a user attempts to deploy a code package, Lambda performs validation checks on the code package before accepting the deployment.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Package the source code in a zip file and upload it to an S3 bucket.**
    
    **– Sign the code using an AWS Signer profile.**
    
    The option that says: **Bundle the source code in a folder named `lambda_handler` and upload it to an S3 bucket** is incorrect. AWS Signer requires Lambda functions to be packaged in a ZIP file.
    
    The option that says: **Digitally sign the code using an AWS KMS asymmetric key. Restrict access to the KMS key to the security engineer** is incorrect. Although you can use KMS asymmetric keys for digital signing, the code signing feature in Lambda only works with AWS Signer.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon CodeGuru to detect security vulnerabilities in the code** is incorrect. Scanning for malicious code and verifying the authenticity of the code are two different things. The former involves finding threats that could be exploited by attackers, whereas the latter ensures that the code has not been tampered with or altered in any way before it is deployed to a Lambda function.
    

A security engineer plans to set up AWS CloudTrail in order to record and monitor all AWS API operations across multiple AWS accounts. The log files must be stored in a central repository and secured against unauthorized modifications.

Which of the following options will satisfy this requirement? (Select TWO.)

- Enable AWS Organizations for all AWS accounts and create a Service Control Policy to enable AWS CloudTrail in each member account. Configure the organization trail to write logs directly to the Amazon S3 bucket.
- Enable server access logging on the Amazon S3 bucket.
- Use Amazon Inspector to monitor the Amazon S3 bucket policy.
- **Set up an Amazon S3 bucket in the logging account and authorize AWS CloudTrail to write logs to this bucket in the member accounts.**
- **Enable AWS Organizations across all AWS accounts and log file validation for AWS CloudTrail. Configure the organization trail to write logs directly to the Amazon S3 bucket.**
- **Evaluation**
    
    **AWS CloudTrail** is a service that enables you to track and monitor activity across your AWS infrastructure by recording API calls and other account activity. By default, CloudTrail logs are stored in an S3 bucket specific to each AWS account. However, you can configure CloudTrail to deliver logs to a central S3 bucket to consolidate and centralize the log storage.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws-cloudtrail-log-file-validation.png)
    
    In order to log all AWS API activity to a central location, you need to provide all AWS accounts the ability to push the logs to a central S3 bucket. You can set up a central Amazon S3 bucket in a dedicated log account to meet the requirement of storing and securing AWS CloudTrail log files across multiple accounts.
    
    To ensure the integrity of these log files and detect any modifications, deletions, or unchanged status, you can enable CloudTrail log file validation. By enabling log file integrity validation when setting up an organization trail within AWS Organizations, you can extend this capability to all accounts within the organization. This organization trail collects activity logs from all organization accounts and stores them in the central S3 bucket.
    
    Importantly, member accounts are restricted from deleting or modifying the organization trail. Only the management account, which oversees the organization, retains the ability to delete or modify the trail associated with the organization.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Set up an Amazon S3 bucket in the logging account and authorize AWS CloudTrail to write logs to this bucket in the member accounts.**
    
    **– Enable AWS Organizations across all AWS accounts and log file validation for AWS CloudTrail. Configure the organization trail to write logs directly to the Amazon S3 bucket.**
    
    The option that says: **Enable AWS Organizations for all AWS accounts and create a Service Control Policy to enable AWS CloudTrail in each member account. Configure the organization trail to write logs directly to the Amazon S3 bucket** is incorrect. While an SCP can be used to grant or deny permissions for specific AWS services, it cannot directly enable or configure CloudTrail and specify the location of CloudTrail logs. The SCP primarily focuses on managing access control policies rather than initiating service-specific actions like enabling CloudTrail and configuring log destinations.
    
    The option that says: **Enable server access logging on the Amazon S3 bucket** is incorrect because this just allows you to track requests made to the S3 bucket. It can be useful for auditing purposes or tracking bucket activity. However, it doesn’t fulfill the requirement of recording and monitoring all AWS API operations across multiple AWS accounts.
    
    The option that says: **Use Amazon Inspector to monitor the Amazon S3 bucket policy** is incorrect because this is not directly related to setting up AWS CloudTrail or securing the log files against unauthorized modifications.
    

A security analyst is developing a Lambda function that analyzes CloudTrail logs located in an S3 bucket. The function searches the CloudTrail logs for root and admin-related activities using Amazon Athena. The results of Athena queries are then saved in the bucket containing the logs. When run through the AWS Console, the Lambda function executes successfully, however, no query results are being delivered to the S3 bucket.

Below are the IAM permissions of the security analyst and the Lambda function’s execution role respectively.

![iam-user-permissions](https://td-portal-cdn.tutorialsdojo.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/10/iam-user-permissions.png)

![lambda-exec role](https://td-portal-cdn.tutorialsdojo.com/wp-content/uploads/2021/10/lambda-exec-role.png)

What is causing the issue?

- The security analyst is forbidden to make any Athena queries.
- The security analyst lacks the `s3:PutObject` permission to write the Athena query results to the S3 bucket.
- **The Lambda function’s execution role has insufficient permissions to access the S3 bucket.**
    
    **Amazon Athena** automatically stores query results and metadata information for each query that runs in a query result location that you can specify in Amazon S3. If necessary, you can access the files in this location to work with them. Since Athena uses IAM policies to restrict access to Athena operations, you must assign enough permissions to whoever is calling the Athena APIs. For example, if you’re using an IAM user to run Athena queries, see to it that you also give sufficient S3 permissions for Athena to write results to an S3 bucket.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-Lambda-Execution-Role-S3.PNG)
    
    In the scenario, the Lambda function does not have the permissions to access the S3 bucket containing the CloudTrail logs. Since the Lambda function is the one initiating the Athena query, its execution role must be given enough S3 permissions to access the S3 bucket.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **The Lambda function’s execution role has insufficient permissions to access the S3 bucket.**
    
    The option that says: **The security analyst lacks the `s3:PutObject` permission to write Athena query results to the S3 bucket** is incorrect. The security analyst is not required to have this permission because he’s not the one running the Athena query.
    
    The option that says: **The security analyst is forbidden to make any Athena queries** is incorrect. Although it’s true that the security analyst does not have any Athena permissions, he does not need to have one in this case since the query is being executed by the Lambda function.
    
    The option that says: **The results of Athena queries must be stored in a separate S3 bucket** is incorrect. This is not true, you can configure Athena to save its output to any S3 buckets as long as it has the permission to do so.
    
- The results of Athena queries must be stored in a separate S3 bucket.

A Solutions Architect is designing an application to fetch the Amazon RDS database credentials that are stored as Secure String parameters in AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store. The Security Administrator has been tasked to handle the integration but always encounters an error message every time encryption or decryption of a Secure String parameter from the AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store is attempted, particularly when using an AWS KMS key.

Which of the following options could be responsible for this issue? (Select TWO.)

- The KMS key used is symmetric.
- **The credentials that the application is using do not have permission to perform the specified action on the KMS key.**
- **The KMS key is disabled.**
- The key alias was specified when creating the secure string parameter in the AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store instead of the `KeyID` parameter of the KMS key.
- In creating the secure string parameter, the `KeyID` parameter of the KMS key was specified in the AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store instead of the KMS key’s Amazon Resource Name (ARN).
- **Evaluation**
    
    With **AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store**, you can create secure string parameters, which are parameters that have a plaintext parameter name and an encrypted parameter value. Parameter Store uses AWS KMS to encrypt and decrypt the parameter values of secure string parameters.
    
    With Parameter Store, you can create, store, and manage data as parameters with values. You can create a parameter in the Parameter Store and use it in multiple applications and services subject to the policies and permissions that you design. When you need to change a parameter value, you change one instance rather than managing error-prone changes to numerous sources. Parameter Store supports a hierarchical structure for parameter names, so you can qualify a parameter for specific uses.
    
    To manage sensitive data, you can create secure string parameters. Parameter Store uses AWS KMS Key to encrypt the parameter values of secure string parameters when you create or change them. It also uses KMS keys to decrypt the parameter values when you access them. You can use the AWS managed KMS key that Parameter Store creates for your account or specify your own customer-managed KMS key. Parameter Store supports only symmetric encryption KMS keys. You cannot use an asymmetric KMS key to encrypt your parameters.
    
    ![AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/service-pstore-standard.png)
    
    To perform any operation on a secure string parameter, the Parameter Store must be able to use the AWS KMS key that you specify for your intended operation. Most of the Parameter Store failures related to KMS keys are caused by the following problems:
    
    **– The credentials that an application is using do not have permission to perform the specified action on the KMS key** – To fix this error, run the application with different credentials or revise the IAM or key policy that is preventing the operation.
    
    **– The KMS key is not found** – This typically happens when you use an incorrect identifier for the KMS key. Find the correct identifiers for the KMS key and try the command again.
    
    **– The KMS key is not enabled**  – When this occurs, Parameter Store returns an InvalidKeyId exception with a detailed error message from AWS KMS. If the KMS key state is Disabled, enable it. If it is Pending Import, complete the import procedure. If the key state is Pending Deletion, cancel the key deletion or use a different KMS key.
    
    To use a customer-managed KMS key instead of the AWS-managed key assigned to your account, you must specify the key by using the `--key-id` parameter. The parameter supports the following KMS parameter formats.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– The credentials that the application is using do not have permission to perform the specified action on the KMS key.**
    
    **– The KMS key is disabled.**
    
    The option that says: **The KMS key used is symmetric** is incorrect because the AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store actually supports symmetric encryption KMS keys. However, you cannot use an asymmetric KMS key to encrypt your parameters.
    
    The option that says: **In creating the secure string parameter, the `KeyID` parameter of the KMS key was specified in the AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store instead of the KMS key’s Amazon Resource Name (ARN)** is incorrect because the `KeyID` parameter is primarily used in creating the secure string parameter in AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store. This is an unlikely cause of the issue.
    
    The option that says: **The key alias was specified when creating the secure string parameter in the AWS Systems Manager Parameter Store instead of the `KeyID` parameter of the KMS key** is incorrect because you can also use the key alias of the KMS key and not just the `KeyID` parameter of the KMS key.
    

A company has experienced an incident where its service becomes inaccessible to some users. The system administrator searched the application and server logs for errors but found nothing that could have brought the server down. It was later found that the issue was caused by misconfigured DNS settings in Route 53. Management has decided to log public DNS queries to help debug similar issues in the future.

How can the administrator achieve this?

- Activate Route 53 DNS query logging. Specify an Amazon S3 bucket as the destination.
- **Activate Route 53 DNS query logging. Specify a CloudWatch Logs log group as the destination.**
    
    You can configure Amazon Route 53 to log information about the public DNS queries that Route 53 receives, such as the following:
    
    - Domain or subdomain that was requested
    - Date and time of the request
    - DNS record type (such as A or AAAA)
    - Route 53 edge location that responded to the DNS query
    - DNS response code, such as `NoError` or `ServFail`
    
    Amazon Route 53 sends query logs directly to CloudWatch Logs; the logs are never accessible through Route 53. Instead, you use CloudWatch Logs to view logs in near real-time, search and filter data, and export logs to Amazon S3.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/route-53-dns-query-log-sample.jpg)
    
    ```
    
    ```
    
    In the scenario, the misconfigured DNS settings prevent a client from resolving the DNS records it needs to connect to the application. If a client cannot resolve the IP address of the application, it won’t be able to connect to the application’s server. This can cause the application to appear unresponsive, even if its backend servers are working properly. To avoid these issues, it’s important to monitor the performance of your DNS server and ensure that it is always available and responsive. One way of monitoring it is by enabling Route 53 DNS query logging.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is to **Activate Route 53 DNS query logging. Specify a CloudWatch Logs log group as the destination.**
    
    The option that says: **Activate Route 53 DNS query logging. Specify an Amazon S3 bucket as the destination** is incorrect. Public DNS query logs can only be published to Amazon CloudWatch Logs.
    
    Although both Amazon CloudWatch Logs and Amazon S3 are valid destinations for Route 53 Resolver query logs, configuring a Route 53 Resolver endpoint is unnecessary because this feature is designed for DNS resolution within and between VPCs and on-premises networks. Take note that the application is publicly accessible. Therefore, the following options are both incorrect:
    
    **– Activate Route 53 Resolver query logging. Specify a CloudWatch Logs log group as the destination.**
    
    **– Activate Route 53 Resolver query logging. Specify an Amazon S3 bucket as the destination**.
    
- Activate Route 53 Resolver query logging. Specify a CloudWatch Logs log group as the destination.
- Activate Route 53 Resolver query logging. Specify an Amazon S3 bucket as the destination.

An organization plans to automate and standardize the deployment of infrastructure using AWS CloudFormation templates. The `AWS::ImageBuilder::ImageRecipe` Image Builder image recipe is used to define validation and security hardening steps for the templates in CloudFormation.

The Solutions Architect needs to ensure that users are unable to modify the physical IDs or remove the resources generated by the template. The ability to modify resources should be restricted to users with administrative privileges only.

Which combination of actions should be implemented to fulfill this requirement? (Select TWO.)

- **Enable termination protection.**
- Perform drift detection operation on the stack.
- **Create a Stack Policy to deny the action `Update:Replace` and `Update:Delete`.**
- Create a Stack Policy to deny the action `Update:Modify`.
- Create a Stack Policy to deny the action `Update:*`.
- **Evaluation**
    
    **AWS CloudFormation** is a service that enables you to automate the deployment and management of infrastructure resources. It allows you to define your infrastructure as code using templates written in JSON or YAML, which can be version controlled and easily replicated. CloudFormation templates describe the desired state of your infrastructure, including resources such as EC2 instances, databases, and networking components. By using CloudFormation, you can provision and update your entire infrastructure stack in a consistent and repeatable manner, simplifying resource management and ensuring infrastructure consistency.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-CloudFormation-Stack-Policy-Termination-Protection.png)
    
    Enabling termination protection for a resource ensures that it cannot be accidentally deleted or terminated, providing an extra layer of protection against unintended removal when making updates or changes to the CloudFormation stack.
    
    When you create a stack policy, you have the ability to specify the update actions that you wish to permit or restrict, allowing you to have granular control over the modifications made to your stack.
    
    1. **Update:Modify** – this action refers to update operations where resources can undergo either no interruptions or some interruptions while changes are applied. The resources retain their physical ID during the update.
    
    2. **Update:Replace** – this action is used for update operations that require recreating resources. AWS CloudFormation creates a new resource with the specified updates and then deletes the old resource. As a result, the new resource might have a different physical ID.
    
    3. **Update:Delete** – this action is used for update operations that involve removing resources. When a resource is completely removed from a stack template, this action is required.
    
    4. **Update:*** – this action represents all possible update actions. The asterisk acts as a wildcard, encompassing any type of update operation.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is:
    
    **– Enable termination protection.**
    
    **– Create a Stack Policy to deny the action `Update:Replace` and `Update:Delete`.**
    
    The option that says: **Create a Stack Policy to deny the action `Update:*`** is incorrect because this would prevent all updates and restrict users with administrative privileges from modifying the stack. Only non-admin users who will update or remove resources from the stack should be restricted in this scenario.
    
    The option that says: **Create a Stack Policy to deny the action `Update:Modify`** is incorrect because this option would allow non-admin users to remove resources in the stack.
    
    The option that says: **Perform drift detection operation on the stack** is incorrect because this just identifies any differences between the desired state and the actual state of the resources in the stack. You don’t need to implement this feature since the requirement is to prevent non-admin users from modifying or deleting the resource.
    

A startup is running its container-based application on AWS using AWS Fargate. The deployed application is in multiple application accounts (dev, qa, uat, and prod). The Security team must make sure that the container images are free from severe vulnerabilities. Additionally, the team must implement secure access controls to only allow the application accounts and specific roles access to the container images.

Which solution will satisfy the requirements with the LEAST operational overhead?

- Retrieve container images from a public container registry. Upload the acquired images to Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repositories, configuring "scan on push" in a centralized AWS account. Implement Amazon ECR cross-account replication from the centralized AWS account to the target AWS accounts (e.g., dev, qa, uat, prod).
- **Retrieve container images from a public container registry. Upload the acquired images to Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repositories, configuring "scan on push" in a centralized AWS account. Implement repository policies and identity-based policies to govern access, restricting entry to designated IAM principals and AWS accounts authorized to access the images.**
    
    **Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR)** is an AWS managed container image registry service that is secure, scalable, and reliable. Amazon ECR supports private repositories with resource-based permissions using AWS IAM. This is so that specified users or Amazon EC2 instances can access your container repositories and images.
    
    Amazon ECR provides basic scanning type which uses the Common Vulnerabilities and Exposures (CVEs) database from the open-source Clair project. With basic scanning enabled on your private registry, you can configure repository filters to specify which repositories are set to scan on push or you can perform manual scans. Amazon ECR provides a list of scan findings. Each container image may be scanned once per 24 hours.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/ECR_Repo_policy_BJL_25072023.png)
    
    In order to allow other accounts to push or pull images from the centralized account repositories. The repository must allow the other accounts by adding repository policies. Ensure that the application accounts have the necessary ECR permissions.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Retrieve container images from a public container registry. Upload the acquired images to Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repositories, configuring “scan on push” in a centralized AWS account. Implement repository policies and identity-based policies to govern access, restricting entry to designated IAM principals and AWS accounts authorized to access the images.**
    
    The option that says: **Retrieve container images from a public container registry. Upload the acquired images to Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repositories, configuring “scan on push” in a centralized AWS account. Implement Amazon ECR cross-account replication from the centralized AWS account to the target AWS accounts (e.g., dev, qa, uat, prod)** is incorrect. While this can allow the images from the centralized account to copy images to other accounts, it does not include copying the repository settings. The scanning option for a repository is off by default. Therefore configuring “scan on push” in a centralized AWS account is not replicated to other accounts. The security team will have to manually enable the scanning to all of the application account repositories.
    
    The option that says: **Retrieve container images from a public container registry. Upload the acquired images to Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repositories, configuring “scan on push” in a centralized AWS account. Use CI/CD pipeline to deploy images to other accounts. Create an Amazon EventBridge rule for `PutImage` that targets lambda to trigger CI/CD pipelines on other accounts** is incorrect. Although this will allow the application accounts to use a copy of the image from the centralized account, configuring a CI/CD for each application account and Amazon EventBridge rule is unnecessary to access the images from the centralized account. This is NOT the best approach considering the operational overhead. The security team only needs to configure the repository policies to allow other application accounts.
    
    The option that says: **Retrieve container images from a public container registry. Upload the acquired images to Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repositories, configuring “scan on push” in a centralized AWS account. Implement repository policies and attributes-based access control (ABAC) to govern access, restricting entry to designated IAM principals and AWS accounts authorized to access the images** is incorrect. Even though the company has a multiple account setup, the scenario did not mention anything about IAM Identity Center (successor to AWS Single Sign-On). The security team can only implement attributes-based access control (ABAC) using IAM Identity Center.
    
- Retrieve container images from a public container registry. Upload the acquired images to Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repositories, configuring "scan on push" in a centralized AWS account. Implement repository policies and attributes-based access control (ABAC) to govern access, restricting entry to designated IAM principals and AWS accounts authorized to access the images.
- Retrieve container images from a public container registry. Upload the acquired images to Amazon Elastic Container Registry (Amazon ECR) repositories, configuring “scan on push” in a centralized AWS account. Use CI/CD pipeline to deploy images to other accounts. Create an Amazon EventBridge rule for `PutImage` that targets lambda to trigger CI/CD pipelines on other accounts.

A security engineer was notified via email by the AWS Trust & Safety team about an abuse report. The report highlights a potentially compromised IAM access key that previously belonged to a former DevOps engineer of the company.

From the initial investigation, the access key is used in an AWS Lambda function that sends emails even though it is not supposed to. This access key enables the developer to launch various AWS resources across different AWS regions by using the AWS CloudFormation StackSets API. The stacks were generated by invoking the Lambda Function URL and are used for unauthorized cryptocurrency mining.

The security engineer must respond to the abuse report, ensure that the compromised access key is rendered ineffective, and implement security best practices.

Which solution will meet the requirements?

- **Delete the compromised IAM access key. Cease the use of the IAM access key. Configure a new IAM role for the Lambda function’s intended use. Assign the IAM role to the Lambda. Directly respond to the abuse report message to detail these remediation steps.**
    
    The AWS Trust & Safety Team sends abuse reports to the security contact on your account. If there is no security contact listed, then the AWS Trust & Safety Team contacts you using the email address listed on your account. The AWS Trust & Safety team can assist you when AWS resources are implicated in the following abuse types:
    
    – Web content/non-copyright intellectual property
    
    – Email abuse
    
    – Network activity
    
    – Copyright
    
    If you receive an abuse notice from AWS, do the following:
    
    – Review the abuse notice to see what content or activity was reported. Logs that implicate abuse are included along with the abuse report, as provided by the reporter.
    
    – Reply directly to the abuse report and explain how you’re preventing the abusive activity from recurring in the future. Note: If you don’t respond to an abuse notice within 24 hours, AWS might block your resources or suspend your AWS account.
    
    In many scenarios, you don’t need long-term access keys that never expire (as you have with an IAM user). Instead, you can create IAM roles and generate temporary security credentials. Temporary security credentials consist of an access key ID and a secret access key, but they also include a security token that indicates when the credentials expire.
    
    You can either deactivate or delete the compromised access key in your AWS Management Console as shown below to immediately stop any unauthorized access:
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/deactivate_compromised_iam_access_key_sept_2023.png)
    
    It is best practice to not use access keys directly in your application. Don’t pass access keys to the application, embed them in the application, or let the application read access keys from any source. Instead, define an IAM role that has appropriate permissions for your application.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Delete the compromised IAM access key. Cease the use of the IAM access key. Configure a new IAM role for the Lambda function’s intended use. Assign the IAM role to the Lambda. Directly respond to the abuse report message to detail these remediation steps.**
    
    The option that says: **Deactivate the compromised IAM access key. Generate a new access key. Apply the new access key to the Lambda. Update the permission of the IAM user to only grant the least privileged access. Create a new case for AWS Support to detail these remediation steps** is incorrect. Although this implementation will improve security and remove the compromised access key, it still uses a long-term access key, which is not a best practice. It is better to use an IAM role to the Lambda function instead and grant the least privileged access. Also, you can directly respond to the abuse report instead of creating an AWS Support ticket.
    
    The option that says: **Delete the compromised IAM access key. Generate a new access key. Apply the new access key to the Lambda. Deploy Trusted Advisor Exposed Keys Event Monitor solution to avoid future compromise of IAM access keys. Directly respond to the abuse report message to detail these remediation steps** is incorrect. While removing the compromised IAM access key is a step in the right direction, deploying the Trusted Advisor Exposed Keys Event Monitor solution will not directly help in the prevention of compromised IAM access keys from recurring. Trusted Advisor Exposed Keys Event Monitor is an automated solution to monitor and remediate exposed access keys and not for compromised access keys.
    
    The option that says: **Remove the compromised IAM access key. Generate a new access key. Store the access key to AWS Secrets Manager as a secret. Configure the Lambda to retrieve the access key from Secrets Manager. Create a new case for AWS Support to detail these remediation steps** is incorrect. Even though using AWS Secrets Manager is a good practice for storing secrets such as database credentials, using the access key from the Lambda function, in general, is not necessary. You can attach an IAM role to the Lambda instead, so you won’t need to use access keys anymore, which is quite vulnerable from being compromised.
    
- Deactivate the compromised IAM access key. Generate a new access key. Apply the new access key to the Lambda. Update the permission of the IAM user to only grant the least privileged access. Create a new case for AWS Support to detail these remediation steps.
- Remove the compromised IAM access key. Generate a new access key. Store the access key to AWS Secrets Manager as a secret. Configure the Lambda to retrieve the access key from Secrets Manager. Create a new case for AWS Support to detail these remediation steps.
- Delete the compromised IAM access key. Generate a new access key. Apply the new access key to the Lambda. Deploy Trusted Advisor Exposed Keys Event Monitor solution to avoid future compromise of IAM access keys. Directly respond to the abuse report message to detail these remediation steps.

A financial services company operates a web platform handling critical financial transactions on Amazon EC2 instances within a VPC. The security team must monitor DNS query activities for timely security analysis. The security team needs to compile a list of frequently occurring DNS queries over some time.

Which solution can meet these requirements?

- Install the Amazon CloudWatch agent and configure BIND DNS to an Amazon EC2 instance within the VPC. Direct the BIND logs to an Amazon CloudWatch Logs log group. Utilize Amazon CloudWatch Contributor Insights to analyze the data and generate time series to compile a list of the most frequently occurring DNS queries.
- Enable VPC Flow logs. Direct the logs to an Amazon CloudWatch Logs log group. Utilize Amazon CloudWatch Contributor Insights to analyze the data and generate time series to compile a list of the most frequently occurring DNS queries.
- Enable Amazon Route 53 Resolver query logging. Direct the logs to an Amazon CloudWatch Logs log group. Export logs to S3. Utilize Amazon Athena to run SQL queries on the stored logs. Process the results of the Athena queries to compile a list of the most frequently occurring DNS queries.
- **Enable Amazon Route 53 Resolver query logging. Direct the logs to an Amazon CloudWatch Logs log group. Utilize Amazon CloudWatch Contributor Insights to analyze the data and generate time series to compile a list of the most frequently occurring DNS queries.**
    
    **Route 53 Resolver Query Logs** will let you log all DNS queries made by resources within your Amazon Virtual Private Cloud (Amazon VPC). Whether it’s an Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (Amazon EC2) instance, an AWS Lambda function, or a container if it lives in your Amazon VPC and makes a DNS query, then this feature will log it; you are then able to explore and better understand how your applications are operating.
    
    You can send the logs to one of the following AWS resources:
    
    – Amazon CloudWatch Logs (CloudWatch Logs) log group
    
    – Amazon S3 (S3) bucket
    
    – Firehose stream
    
    You can use Contributor Insights to analyze log data and create time series that display contributor data. You can see metrics about the top-N contributors, the total number of unique contributors, and their usage. This helps you find top talkers and understand who or what is impacting system performance.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/Route_53_query_logging_BJL_25072023.png)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Enable Amazon Route 53 Resolver query logging. Direct the logs to an Amazon CloudWatch Logs log group. Utilize Amazon CloudWatch Contributor Insights to analyze the data and generate time series to compile a list of the most frequently occurring DNS queries.**
    
    The option that says: **Enable VPC Flow logs. Direct the logs to an Amazon CloudWatch Logs log group. Utilize Amazon CloudWatch Contributor Insights to analyze the data and generate time series to compile a list of the most frequently occurring DNS queries** is incorrect. Although it is possible to direct VPC flow logs to the CloudWatch logs log group, these logs only capture network flow data, and won’t provide specific DNS query information needed for analysis. Hence, this solution will not satisfy the requirements.
    
    The option that says: **Enable Amazon Route 53 Resolver query logging. Direct the logs to an Amazon CloudWatch Logs log group. Export logs to S3. Utilize Amazon Athena to run SQL queries on the stored logs. Process the results of the Athena queries to compile a list of the most frequently occurring DNS queries** is incorrect. While enabling Amazon Route 53 Resolver query logging is a step in the right direction, using Amazon Athena to run SQL queries on the stored logs is not the most efficient approach for compiling a list of frequently occurring DNS queries over time. An S3 bucket is economical for long-term log archiving, and latency is typically higher, which is undesirable when you want to implement monitoring for DNS query activities for timely security analysis.
    
    The option that says: **Install the Amazon CloudWatch agent and configure BIND DNS to an Amazon EC2 instance within the VPC. Direct the BIND logs to an Amazon CloudWatch Logs log group. Utilize Amazon CloudWatch Contributor Insights to analyze the data and generate time series to compile a list of the most frequently occurring DNS queries** is incorrect. The primary issue with this solution is that it lacks a native and straightforward way to capture DNS query logs in a format readily compatible with CloudWatch Contributor Insights. While you can collect BIND logs using the CloudWatch agent, analyzing DNS queries efficiently with Contributor Insights requires specific log formats and log sources for DNS query logging.
    

An eCommerce company is setting up an email service using Amazon SES to send marketing campaigns to its subscribers. All connections must be TLS-encrypted.

Which email protocol and port number should be included in the SES endpoint? (Select TWO.)

- Port number 995
- Port number 143
- **Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP)**
- **Port number 587**
- Post Office Protocol version 3 (POP3)
- **Evaluation**
    
    The Amazon SES SMTP endpoint requires that all connections be encrypted using Transport Layer Security (TLS). (Note that TLS is often referred to by the name of its predecessor protocol, SSL.) Amazon SES supports two mechanisms for establishing a TLS-encrypted connection: STARTTLS and TLS Wrapper.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-SES-SMTP-Settings.PNG)
    
    STARTTLS is a means of upgrading an unencrypted connection to an encrypted connection. To set up a STARTTLS connection, the SMTP client connects to the Amazon SES SMTP endpoint on **port 25, 587, or 2587**, issues an EHLO command, and waits for the server to announce that it supports the STARTTLS SMTP extension. The client then issues the STARTTLS command, initiating TLS negotiation. When negotiation is complete, the client issues an EHLO command over the new encrypted connection, and the SMTP session proceeds normally.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Simple Mail Transfer Protocol (SMTP)**
    
    **– Port number 587**
    
    **Post Office Protocol version 3 (POP3)** is incorrect. This protocol is used for receiving emails and not for sending. Additionally, Amazon SES does not have a POP server.
    
    **Port number 995** is incorrect as this port number is reserved for encrypted POP3 connections.
    
    **Port number 143** is incorrect. This port number is used for Internet Message Access Protocol (IMAP) connections.
    

A security administrator must see to it that products deployed in AWS Service Catalog can be safely launched by end-users without them having to use their IAM credentials to enforce least privilege access.

Which solution should the security administrator employ?

- Set a notification constraint to alert the administrator about product-related stack events.
- Set a stack set constraint to individual products in a portfolio.
- **Apply launch constraints to individual products in a portfolio.**
    
    A launch constraint specifies the AWS Identity and Access Management (IAM) role that AWS Service Catalog assumes when an end-user launches a product. Launch constraints apply to products in the portfolio and do not apply at the portfolio level or to a product across all portfolios. To associate a launch constraint with all products in a portfolio, you must apply the launch constraint to each product individually.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-ServiceCatalog-Launch-Constraint.PNG)
    
    Without a launch constraint, end users must launch and manage products using their own IAM credentials. To do so, they must have permissions for AWS CloudFormation, AWS services that the products use, and AWS Service Catalog. By using a launch role, you can instead limit the end users’ permissions to the minimum they require for that product.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Apply launch constraints to individual products in a portfolio.**
    
    The option that says: **Apply template constraints to individual products in a portfolio** is incorrect. This will only limit the allowable parameter values defined in the CloudFormation template that supports a product.
    
    The option that says: **Set a stack set constraint to individual products in a portfolio** is incorrect. This is useful if you want to specify which accounts and regions can launch a product and in what order they should be deployed.
