    The option that says: **Set a notification constraint to alert the administrator about product-related stack events** is incorrect. This has nothing to do with restricting the IAM permissions of end-users.
    
- Apply template constraints to individual products in a portfolio.

An eCommerce company has an application running on AWS and plans to perform DDoS testing on Amazon EC2 instances. This is to verify the performance of the AWS Shield Advanced service that the company uses. The Security Team has been notified about this activity and informed the company that it would violate the AWS Customer Agreement.

Which of the following options can be implemented while adhering to the terms of the agreement?

- Create an AWS CloudFormation template and provision new resources in a different AWS Region. Use the Network Access Analyzer to simulate a DDoS attack.
- Install Amazon Inspector Agents on Amazon EC2 instances and perform the DDoS simulation using a combination of Amazon EC2 Instance Connect and Systems Manager Automation.
- Install Amazon SSM Agents on Amazon EC2 instances. Create an Amazon CloudWatch alarm and perform the DDoS simulation.
- **Perform the simulation with the help of an APN Partner.**
    
    The **AWS Partner Network (APN)** is a global community of consulting firms, technology providers, and independent software vendors (ISVs) who collaborate with Amazon Web Services (AWS) to deliver innovative solutions and services to customers. APN partners receive various resources, support, and training from AWS to help them build, market, and sell their offerings on the AWS platform.
    
    ![](http://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-DDOS-Simulation-Testing-Form.PNG)
    
    DDoS attacks occur when an attacker uses a flood of traffic from multiple sources to try to disrupt the availability of a targeted application. DDoS simulation testing employs a controlled DDoS attack to allow the owner of an application to assess the application’s resilience and practice event response.
    
    DDoS simulation testing is permitted on AWS, subject to the terms and conditions listed below:
    
    – All testing is subject to the terms of the [**AWS Customer Agreement**](https://aws.amazon.com/agreement/) or any other agreement governing your purchase and use of Amazon Web Services.
    
    – DDoS simulation testing must be performed by an **AWS Partner Network (APN)** Partner that has been pre-approved by AWS to conduct DDoS simulation tests (AWS DDoS Test Partner).
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Perform the simulation with the help of an APN Partner.**
    
    The option that says: **Install Amazon Inspector Agents on Amazon EC2 instances and perform the DDoS simulation** is incorrect because Amazon Inspector is not a DDoS simulation tool. The agent is primarily used for vulnerability assessment and security monitoring of Amazon EC2 instances.
    
    The option that says: **Install Amazon SSM Agents on Amazon EC2 instances. Create an Amazon CloudWatch alarm and perform the DDoS simulation using a combination of Amazon EC2 Instance Connect and Systems Manager Automation** is incorrect. Just like the option above, this option will not change the outcome and still violate the AWS Customer Agreement. Using Amazon EC2 Instance Connect can’t simulate a DDoS attack as this feature only provides a simple and secure way to connect to your Linux instances using Secure Shell (SSH).
    
    The option that says: **Create an AWS CloudFormation template and provision new resources in a different AWS Region. Use the Network Access Analyzer to simulate a DDoS attack** is incorrect because even though you created the resources in a different AWS Region, this will still violate the AWS Customer Agreement. You need to ask for APN Partner assistance to conduct the DDoS simulation. In addition, the Network Access Analyzer is a feature that identifies unintended network access to your resources on AWS and is not used to simulate a DDoS attack.
    

An IT consulting firm plans to generate a weekly report to monitor the clients’ AWS and on-premises servers that lack the latest security patches. As part of the initial setup, CloudWatch and SSM agents were installed on each server. The Security team was assigned the task of implementing a solution to ensure all servers have up-to-date security patches and can achieve compliance within 24 hours.

How can the Security team fulfill this requirement?

- Use a combination of AWS Audit Manager and AWS Config custom rule to track the non-compliant server and redeploy it using the latest AMI.
- Use AWS License Manager to track software licenses and generate a report.
- Use AWS CloudTrail to track non-compliant servers and trigger a Lambda function to automatically redeploy resources using the latest AMI.
- **Use AWS Systems Manager to create a patch policy and compliance report.**
    
    **AWS Systems Manager – Patch Manager** is a service that helps automate the process of managing patches for Amazon Elastic Compute Cloud (EC2) instances and on-premises servers. It enables system administrators to simplify patch management, improve security, and maintain compliance across their infrastructure.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/AWS-SSM-PatchManager.png)
    
    In this scenario, the Security team can use Patch Manager to do the following:
    
    1. Create a **patch policy** to define whether to conduct scanning only or also install missing patches. Utilize this policy to deploy patches across multiple AWS accounts simultaneously.
    
    2. Access a comprehensive **dashboard** of patch compliance for your applications and nodes, providing a high-level view of their current patch status.
    
    3. Review detailed **reports** highlighting resources that do not adhere to your patch policies. Gain insights into the specific areas of non-compliance and identify the necessary actions to address them.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use AWS Systems Manager to create a patch policy and compliance report.**
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS License Manager to track software licenses and generate a report** is incorrect because software licenses are different from security patches. The requirement from this scenario is to install or apply the latest security patches on all servers.
    
    The option that says: **Use AWS CloudTrail to track non-compliant servers and trigger a Lambda function to automatically redeploy resources using the latest AMI** is incorrect because CloudTrail does not provide reports for non-compliant servers. Also, redeploying the AMI with the latest patches may also be impractical due to the time-consuming process involved.
    
    The option that says: **Use a combination of AWS Audit Manager and AWS Config custom rule to track the non-compliant server and redeploy it using the latest AMI** is incorrect because AWS Config cannot track on-premises servers. It also does not have the capability to directly redeploy EC2 instances. Although the AWS Audit Manager can audit your on-premises resources, using the AWS Systems Manager service is a better choice as it provides more capabilities to track the patches of your compute resources.
    

A company has an application that uses multiple buckets in Amazon S3 to store sensitive documents. Each bucket is SSE-KMS encrypted with customer-managed keys (CMKs).

The Security Team has flagged some CMKs as noncompliant because the rotation was disabled. As a remediation, the Security Engineer enabled automatic KMS Key rotation every year, but the organization still needs to confirm that the key rotations are indeed happening. An email notification must be sent immediately once a CMK has been successfully rotated.

What is the MOST operationally efficient way to achieve this?

- Create an Amazon EventBridge scheduled rule that runs every 30 days and select AWS Lambda as the target. Use the AWS CDK via the `aws-cdk-lib` module in AWS Lambda and call the `listKeys` and `getKeyRotationStatus` with Key ID to monitor all key rotation events.
- **Create an Amazon EventBridge rule with an event pattern for `KMS CMK Rotation`. Select SNS as the target of the event rule.**
    
    AWS Key Management Service (AWS KMS) is a managed service that makes it easy for you to create and control the cryptographic keys that are used to protect your data. AWS KMS integrates with Amazon EventBridge to notify you of important events that affect your KMS keys. Each event is represented in JSON (JavaScript Object Notation) and includes the event name, the date and time when the event occurred, and the affected. You can collect these events and establish rules that route them to one or more targets, such as AWS Lambda functions, Amazon SNS topics, Amazon SQS queues, streams in Amazon Kinesis Data Streams, or built-in targets.
    
    You can use Amazon EventBridge (formerly Amazon CloudWatch Events) to alert you to the following important events in the lifecycle of your KMS keys.
    
    – The key material in a KMS key was automatically rotated.
    
    – The imported key material in a KMS key expired.
    
    – A KMS key that had been scheduled for deletion was deleted.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/public/kms_rotation_event_pattern_BJL_19062023.png)
    
    You can create an Amazon EventBridge rule using this event pattern to detect KMS Key Rotations. Then configure Amazon SNS as the target to send a notification email to the Security Engineer.
    
    When AWS KMS automatically rotates the key material for an AWS-managed key or customer-managed key, it writes a **KMS CMK Rotation** event to Amazon EventBridge and a RotateKey event to your AWS CloudTrail log. You can use these records to verify that the KMS key was rotated.
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Create an Amazon EventBridge rule with an event pattern for `KMS CMK Rotation`. Select SNS as the target of the event rule.**
    
    The option that says: **Create an Amazon EventBridge scheduled rule that runs every 30 days and select AWS Lambda as the target. Use the AWS CDK via the `aws-cdk-lib` module in AWS Lambda and call the `listKeys` and `getKeyRotationStatus` with Key ID to monitor all key rotation events** is incorrect. Although this will let you know the rotation status of all the CMKs, it won’t provide you the information about the rotated keys at the expected time frame since the solution only runs once a month (30 days).
    
    The option that says: **Search the AWS CloudTrail logs using the Event History console to identify the `RotateKey` event name. Compare each event time to each of the CMK’s creation dates** is incorrect. Searching through CloudTrail logs and comparing the dates individually is, albeit valid, not the most efficient way to the meet the requirements. This solution entails a lot of manual overhead and lacks the capability to send an email notification immediately once a CMK has been successfully rotated.
    
    The option that says: **Configure S3 Event Notifications for `s3:ObjectCreated:Put` to the AWS Cloudtrail logs bucket. Run Lambda for every Key Rotation event to notify the Security Engineer** is incorrect. Though it is true that the CloudTrail logs Rotation Key Events and the logs can be saved within the S3 bucket, the S3 Event notifications feature still won’t be able to read the content of the logs and therefore have no way of knowing whether the logs contain CMK rotation events or not. The best way to achieve this is still to use Amazon EventBridge to track the CMK events.
    
- Search the AWS CloudTrail logs using the Event History console to identify the `RotateKey`event name. Compare each event time to each of the CMK’s creation dates.
- Configure S3 Event Notifications for `s3:ObjectCreated:Put` to the AWS Cloudtrail logs bucket. Run Lambda for every Key Rotation event to notify the Security Engineer.

A security administrator is developing a solution to encrypt business data stored on a fleet of Elastic Block Store (EBS)-backed EC2 instances. As per requirement, the key material must be expired automatically after 30 days.

Which solution meets these requirements?

- Use the AWS Encryption SDK.
- Use an AWS Managed KMS key.
- Use a customer-managed KMS key created with KMS generated key material.
- **Use a customer-managed KMS key created with imported key material.**
    
    When you import key material, you can specify an expiration date. When the key material expires, AWS KMS deletes the key material and the AWS KMS key becomes unusable. You can also delete key material on demand. Whether you wait for the key material to expire or you delete it manually, the effect is the same.
    
    AWS KMS deletes the key material, the KMS key’s key state changes to the pending import, and the KMS key is unusable. To use the KMS key again, you must reimport the same key material. Deleting key material affects the KMS key immediately, but you can reverse the deletion of key material by reimporting the same key material into the KMS key.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws-kms-import-key-material.PNG)
    
    Hence, the correct answer is: **Use a customer-managed KMS key created with imported key material.**
    
    The option that says: **Use an AWS Managed KMS key** is incorrect. Customers don’t have control over AWS Managed KMS keys.
    
    The option that says: **Use a customer-managed KMS key created with KMS generated key material** is incorrect. You can set a schedule to automatically delete customer-managed KMS keys. However, exclusively expiring KMS generated key material is not possible.
    
    The option that says: **Use the AWS Encryption SDK** is incorrect. This is just a client-side cryptographic library. EBS uses AWS KMS for encryption.
    

During a security risk assessment, database credentials and API keys were found exposed in the environment variables of an AWS Fargate application. A security engineer is tasked with ensuring all sensitive data are both encrypted at rest and in transit.

Which combination of steps should the security engineer do to complete the task? (Select THREE.)

- Designate a container instance IAM role with the correct IAM policies that can read and retrieve secrets from Secrets Manager.
- **Call the GetSecretValue API within the application to pull the credentials from AWS Secrets Manager.**
- **Store the credentials as secrets in AWS Secrets Manager.**
- SSH into the AWS Fargate instance, read the credentials from Secrets Manager using the GetSecretValue API, and store it in the environment’s variable.
- **Designate a task execution role with the correct IAM policies that can read and retrieve secrets from Secrets Manager.**
- Store the credentials as a JSON object in Amazon S3 and limit application access through the S3 bucket policy.
- **Evaluation**
    
    The task execution role grants the Amazon ECS container and Fargate agents permission to make AWS API calls on your behalf. The task execution IAM role is required depending on the requirements of your task.
    
    ![](https://media.tutorialsdojo.com/aws-fargate-task-role.JPG)
    
    You can assign a task execution role to your task definition for various purposes and services. For example, if the role’s IAM policy has enough permissions, your Fargate-hosted application can retrieve sensitive data stored in Secrets Manager by calling the `GetSecretValue` API.
    
    Hence, the correct answers are:
    
    **– Store the credentials as secrets in AWS Secrets Manager.**
    
    **– Designate a task execution role with the correct IAM policies that can read and retrieve secrets from Secrets Manager.**
    
    **– Call the `GetSecretValue` API within the application to pull the credentials from AWS Secrets Manager.**
    
    The option that says: **Designate a container instance IAM role with the correct IAM policies that can read and retrieve secrets from Secrets Manager** is incorrect. Fargate does not have container instance IAM roles.
    
    The option that says: **SSH into the Fargate instance, read the credentials from Secrets Manager using the `GetSecretValue` API, and store it in the environment’s variable** is incorrect. Although the EXEC command can be used to SSH into a Fargate-managed container built with an SSH-enabled Docker image, this solution is not secure because the credentials will still end up stored in plaintext within the host’s environment.
    
    The option that says: **Store the credentials as a JSON object in Amazon S3 and limit application access through the S3 bucket policy** is incorrect. Storing credentials in Amazon S3 is not as secure as storing them in Secrets Manager.
